An introduction to Docker for reproducible research, with examples from the R environment Computer Science Errors found factual-error major
Review details
Findings (1)
An archived Docker image cannot preserve exact versions of all involved software across any Docker installation. (major, high confidence)
Tag
factual-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Docker, sections “Docker images: resolving ‘Dependency Hell’” and “Tackling code-rot with image versions”; Conclusions, “Limitations and future developments”
Analysis
The paper claims that an image tarball can be read by any Docker installation and run the exact versions of all software involved. It also states that Docker images share the host's Linux kernel and explicitly acknowledges that this introduces reproducibility limitations. Therefore, the tarball does not determine every software version involved.
Evidence
Move the same archived image to two Docker installations whose hosts use different Linux kernel versions. Under the paper's own description, each container uses its host's kernel, so the two executions involve different kernel software despite using the identical tarball. This violates the claimed preservation of exact versions of all software on any Docker installation.
Paper details

Authors: Carl Boettiger

The paper makes one internally contradicted reproducibility guarantee about archived Docker images.

arXiv · PDF · 1702.03037v1

Reviewed 2026-07-28 06:03:59+00:00 UTC · gpt-5.6-sol (high)
Trajectory Recovery From Ash: User Privacy Is NOT Preserved in Aggregated Mobility Data Computer Science Errors found experimental-errorlogical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (2)
The paper reports incompatible final recovery accuracies for the same 100,000-user operator dataset. (major, high confidence)
Tag
experimental-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Table 2; Sections 5.2 and 5.3, Figures 8(a) and 11(a)
Analysis
Table 2 states that the operator dataset contains exactly 100,000 users. Section 5.2 reports 73% final accuracy on that dataset. Section 5.3 says its scale experiment uses subsets of the same operator dataset and reports about 50% accuracy at 100,000 users. A 100,000-user subset of a dataset containing exactly 100,000 users is the full dataset, so the two stated results cannot both describe the same attack and data as claimed.
Evidence
Operator dataset size = 100,000; full-dataset final accuracy = 73%; sampled-subset accuracy at size 100,000 = approximately 50%. At that sample size the input population is identical, yet the reported accuracies differ by about 23 percentage points.
Pointwise recovery accuracy is incorrectly interpreted as the percentage of complete trajectories recovered. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 5.1, Equation (5), and Section 5.2
Analysis
Equation (5) averages the fraction of matching spatiotemporal points in each trajectory. Section 5.2 nevertheless concludes that 98% accuracy means 98% of nighttime trajectories were correctly recovered and that 91% means 91% of whole trajectories were recovered. The defined metric does not measure the fraction of completely correct trajectories.
Evidence
Consider 100 users over 100 time slots. At two slots, swap every user's assigned point with another user's point while leaving the other 98 slots correct. The aggregate observations remain unchanged and Equation (5) gives 98% accuracy, but every recovered trajectory contains errors, so zero complete trajectories are correctly recovered. Thus 98% pointwise accuracy does not imply 98% trajectory-level recovery.
Paper details

Authors: Fengli Xu, Zhen Tu, Yong Li, Pengyu Zhang, Xiaoming Fu, Depeng Jin

Two demonstrable internal errors were found: incompatible accuracy values for the same 100,000-user experiment and an invalid interpretation of pointwise accuracy as trajectory-level recovery.

Certifying and removing disparate impact Statistics Errors found logical-errormathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (5)
Repairing each attribute separately does not ensure fairness for multivariate data. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 5, pp. 11-12; Theorem 5.1
Analysis
Equalizing every protected-group marginal does not eliminate protected information encoded in cross-attribute dependence.
Evidence
Let X be binary. For X=0 let (Y1,Y2) be (0,0) or (1,1); for X=1 let it be (0,1) or (1,0), each equally likely. Every single-coordinate marginal is identical across X, yet whether Y1 equals Y2 predicts X perfectly.
The claimed one-half-fair and one-fair repaired datasets cannot satisfy the paper's definition of epsilon-fairness. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Definition 4.3 and Theorem 5.1
Analysis
Definition 4.3 requires BER(f)>epsilon for every classifier, but even a constant classifier has BER exactly one half.
Evidence
For f(Y)=0, BER=(Pr[f=0|X=1]+Pr[f=1|X=0])/2=(1+0)/2=1/2. Thus no binary dataset can be one-half-fair under the strict inequality, much less one-fair.
The repair does not strongly preserve rank under the paper's stated definition. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Strong-rank-preservation definition and Theorem 5.1
Analysis
The proof assumes Fx(FA^{-1}(z))=z, which generally requires Fx=FA. Quantile transport instead establishes FA(FA^{-1}(Fx(y)))=Fx(y).
Evidence
The paper's example maps a male score 500 from N(400,50^2) to 625 in N(475,75^2). Under the original male distribution these are z-scores 2 and 4.5, so Fx(500) differs from Fx(625), contradicting the stated requirement.
The SVM example weights do not sum to one as claimed. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Section 4.2, Equation (2)
Analysis
The stated normalization is arithmetically incorrect.
Evidence
With Dj=n/(2nj), summing over n+ positive and n- negative examples gives n+/[2n+]*n + n-/[2n-]*n = n, not 1.
The experimental section reverses the certification guarantee. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Sections 4.2 and 6.1
Analysis
The two descriptions assign opposite conclusions to BER above the certification threshold.
Evidence
Section 4.2 says that above the threshold no classifier exhibits disparate impact; Section 6.1 says above it any classifier will have disparate impact. Figure 2 agrees with Section 4.2.
Paper details

Authors: Michael Feldman, Sorelle Friedler, John Moeller, Carlos Scheidegger, Suresh Venkatasubramanian

Social Influence as Intrinsic Motivation for Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning Computer Science Errors found logical-errormathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (3)
Influence through communication is not guaranteed to avoid non-cooperative influence. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 5, paragraph immediately after Equation 2
Analysis
The paper argues that optional messages and an action policy trained on individual environmental reward prevent non-cooperative influence. Those conditions guarantee neither cooperation nor collective benefit because the paper's SSD definition explicitly permits individually rewarding actions that harm the group.
Evidence
In Harvest, a message can reveal an apple's location. The listener may voluntarily use it to collect the apple for +1 individual reward, while harvesting too quickly prevents regrowth and reduces long-term collective reward. The message is useful to the listener and influences its individually trained policy, satisfying both stated premises, yet facilitates non-cooperative behavior.
The stated speaker-consistency formula is not normalized to the claimed interval [0,1]. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Supplementary Section 10.4.4, Equation 8
Analysis
The formula sums one entropy-based score for every agent, symbol, and action without dividing by the numbers of agents, symbols, or actions. Consequently, its maximum generally exceeds one.
Evidence
For one agent with two equiprobable actions and two equiprobable messages related deterministically one-to-one, every conditional entropy in Equation 8 is zero. The formula evaluates to 0.5(2+2)=2, contradicting the claimed range [0,1]. With five agents it evaluates to 10.
The stated Gini formula is not guaranteed to lie in [0,1] for the paper's rewards. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Supplementary Section 10.5.3, Equation 9
Analysis
The standard bound requires nonnegative values and a positive total, but the paper explicitly permits negative rewards: firing costs -1 and being hit costs -50. No restriction to nonnegative returns is imposed before applying the formula.
Evidence
For five agents with rewards (-50,0,0,0,0), which is compatible with the stated reward system, the numerator is 400 and the denominator is 2×5×(-50)=-500, giving G=-0.8 rather than a value in [0,1]. Rewards (1,-1,0,0,0) make the denominator zero, so G is undefined.
Paper details

Authors: Natasha Jaques, Angeliki Lazaridou, Edward Hughes, Caglar Gulcehre, Pedro A. Ortega, DJ Strouse, Joel Z. Leibo, Nando de Freitas

Three demonstrable internal errors affect the communication argument and two supplementary metrics.

An index to quantify an individual's scientific research valid across disciplines Physics and Astronomy Errors found mathematical-error minor
Review details
Findings (1)
The hI values in Table II are not computed from the displayed Na values using Equation (1). (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Equation (1), page 6; Table II, page 9
Analysis
Equation (1) defines hI=h²/Na(T), and Table II labels Na as the number of authors. Multiple rows instead use an undisclosed denominator Na+1, so the discrepancy cannot be rounding.
Evidence
For Tsallis, Table II gives h=35 and Na=82, implying hI=35²/82=14.94, approximately 14.9, not 14.8. The reported 14.8 matches 35²/83=14.76. Likewise, Berkovits has h=20 and Na=36, which gives 11.11 rather than 10.8; 10.8 matches 20²/37=10.81. Letelier has h=17 and Na=26, which gives 11.12 rather than 10.7; 10.7 matches 17²/27=10.70.
Paper details

Findings (2)
Statistical significance is incorrectly equated with necessity for prediction. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 2, Observation 1; Section 5.2, Table 6
Analysis
A significant regression coefficient establishes conditional association under the fitted model, not that the variable is necessary for accurate prediction.
Evidence
A feature can have a statistically significant but tiny coefficient while removing it leaves every predicted class unchanged; significance therefore does not imply necessity.
High accuracy of one pooled predictor does not imply that respondents use similar reasoning. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 5.2; Section 5.4; Concluding Discussion
Analysis
Correlated properties can let distinct respondent-specific rules produce identical judgments, so pooled predictive accuracy cannot identify a shared heuristic.
Evidence
If relevance always equals reliability, half of respondents can use only relevance and half only reliability; one classifier predicts everyone perfectly despite different reasoning rules.

Findings (1)
The paper states that the adversary is trained by back-propagating the predictor loss through it, but that loss provides no adversary gradient. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 3.2, Model Training, page 3
Analysis
Ly is defined from predictor N's recidivism output and does not depend on adversary A's parameters, so it cannot train A. The adversary must minimize Ld.
Evidence
With adversary parameters theta_A, Ly=Ly(N(x),Y), hence dLy/dtheta_A=0, while Ld(A(N(x)),D) supplies the required gradient.

Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type

Analysis
The pooled variance uses denominator n0+n1-2, but the test is assigned n0-n1-2 degrees of freedom.
Evidence
For n0=n1=10, the stated rule gives -2 degrees of freedom, so the prescribed t-test is undefined. The pooled two-sample construction shown immediately beforehand has n0+n1-2=18 residual degrees of freedom.
The two-group proportions statistic always has a zero numerator. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 10, Section 4.1.3, Difference in proportions for two groups
Analysis
The numerator subtracts p(y+|s0) from itself rather than comparing s0 with s1.
Evidence
The displayed statistic is z=[p(y+|s0)-p(y+|s0)]/sigma=0 whenever sigma is finite. Thus even p(y+|s0)=0.9 and p(y+|s1)=0.1 produces z=0, contrary to the stated purpose of testing equality between the groups.
The many-group chi-square statistic rejects equal proportions solely because group sizes differ. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 10, Section 4.1.4, Difference in proportions for many groups
Analysis
The formula compares each group size ni with n times that group's positive rate, rather than comparing observed and expected counts within that group.
Evidence
Let n=100, n1=30, n2=70, and let both groups have p(y+|si)=0.5, exactly satisfying the null. The stated statistic is (30-50)^2/0.5+(70-50)^2/0.5=1600 instead of zero.

Evidence

Findings (2)
The stated eigenvector-centrality equation incorrectly multiplies by the largest eigenvalue. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 5, Methods, Network Measures, Eigenvector definition
Analysis
The paper states e_i = λ∑_j x_ij e_j while defining λ as the largest eigenvalue. The eigenvector equation instead requires division by that eigenvalue, equivalently λe_i = ∑_j x_ij e_j.
Evidence
For a three-node complete graph, the largest eigenvalue is 2 and symmetry requires e_i=c≠0 for every node. The paper's equation gives c=2(c+c)=4c, implying c=0 and therefore no nonzero eigenvector centrality.

arXiv · PDF · 1912.06923v2

Reviewed 2026-07-28 05:54:59+00:00 UTC · gpt-5.6-sol (high)
Identifying and Correcting Label Bias in Machine Learning Computer Science Errors found experimental-errorlogical-errormathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (5)
Proposition 1 is false without an interior-feasibility assumption. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Assumption 1 and Proposition 1, Section 3
Analysis
Assumption 1 permits boundary-valued optimizers, but finite exponential tilting preserves positive support and cannot produce them.
Evidence
Let X={a,b}, P(a)=P(b)=1/2, y_true(a)=y_true(b)=1/2, and use the paper's demographic-parity constraint for G={a}: c(a,1)=1, c(b,1)=-1, and c(x,0)=0. Then y_true is unbiased. Set epsilon=1/2. The constraint (q(a)-q(b))/2=1/2 uniquely forces y_bias(a)=1 and y_bias(b)=0, so it is the constrained KL minimizer. Proposition 1 would require q(a)=1/(1+exp(lambda)) and q(b)=exp(lambda)/(1+exp(lambda)), which are strictly between 0 and 1 for every finite lambda in R. Thus no coefficients asserted by the proposition exist.
Algorithm 1 does not use the weights implied by the paper's demographic-parity constraint. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 2.2, Corollary 1, Section 4.2, Algorithm 1
Analysis
The algorithm is explicitly described as computing Proposition 1's closed-form weights, but it drops both the normalization and population-wide term in the stated constraint.
Evidence
For one group, the paper defines c(x,1)=g(x)/Z_G-1 and c(x,0)=0. Corollary 1 therefore gives positive-label weight sigma(lambda(g/Z_G-1)). Algorithm 1 instead uses sigma(lambda g). With Z_G=1/2 and g=0, the theoretical weight is sigma(-lambda), whereas Algorithm 1 always gives 1/2. These differ for every nonzero lambda and cannot be reconciled by per-example scaling because their positive-to-negative weight ratios differ.
Theorem 2's proof uses a false lower bound on the weights. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix D, proof of Theorem 2, inequality following Equation (3)
Analysis
The proof bounds min{w(x),1-w(x)} using only K and the coefficient bound Lambda, although no bound on the constraint values c_k is assumed.
Evidence
Take K=1, Lambda=lambda=1, and a demographic-parity group with Z_G=0.01. For a group member, c(x,1)=1/Z_G-1=99, so w(x)=sigma(99) and min{w,1-w}=sigma(-99). The proof claims this is at least exp(-1)/(1+exp(-1))=sigma(-1), which is false. This example can satisfy Assumptions 1 and 2 by taking a two-point compact domain, constant y_true=1/2, and defining y_bias by the stated exponential tilt.
Theorem 2's proof concludes a population bound from only an empirical error bound. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix D, final steps of the proof of Theorem 2
Analysis
The theorem concerns E_P[(h*-y_true)^2], but the proof's final controlled quantity is n^{-1} sum_i (h*(x_i)-y_true(x_i))^2. No uniform-convergence or sample-to-population argument is supplied before claiming the theorem follows.
Evidence
Empirical control alone does not imply the stated population control: for any finite sample S from [0,1], h(x)=min(2L dist(x,S),1) is 2L-Lipschitz, vanishes on every sampled point, and nevertheless has positive population squared error relative to y_true=0 under a continuous P. Thus the proof's final inference requires an additional argument not established by its preceding inequalities.

Obtaining fairness using optimal transport theory Mathematics Errors found mathematical-error majorminor

Findings (3)
Theorem 4.3's bound on information loss after repair is false. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.1, Theorem 4.3, equation (4.5), and its Appendix proof
Analysis
The theorem ignores predictive information carried directly by S. Its proof improperly compares classifiers using only the repaired variable with the Bayes classifier g_B(x,s), which depends on S and is therefore not an admissible repaired-data classifier.
Evidence
Let S be Bernoulli(1/2), let X be uniform on [0,1] independently of S, set Y=S, and take T_0=T_1=Id. The transformed conditional distributions are identical, as required. Moreover, η_0(x)=0 and η_1(x)=1 are Lipschitz with any positive stated constants. The Bayes risk using (X,S) is 0, while every classifier using only the repaired variable X has risk 1/2 because X is independent of Y. Thus E(X-tilde)=1/2. Every Wasserstein term on the right of (4.5) is zero, so the theorem asserts 1/2 ≤ 0.
Theorem 2.2 is false for the paper's stated general classifier family G. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 2, Theorem 2.2, and its Appendix proof
Analysis
The proof minimizes over all measurable classifiers by constructing a Bayes classifier, but the theorem only assumes an unspecified family G and never requires that this Bayes classifier belong to G.
Evidence
Let S and U be independent Bernoulli(1/2), set X=(S,U), and let G contain only g(x_1,x_2)=x_2. The conditional laws of X given S=0 and S=1 have disjoint supports, so their total-variation distance is 1 and the theorem's right side is 0. However, a(g)=b(g)=1/2 and BER(g,X,S)=[P(U=0|S=1)+P(U=1|S=0)]/2=1/2. Hence the stated minimum is 1/2, not 0.

Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Section 4.1, displayed derivation immediately after equation (4.5)
Analysis

Evidence

The paper instead first drops the factor 2 and then writes √2 Kπ_0π_1D^2. For π_0=π_1=1/2 and D=1, these are respectively √2K, (√2/2)K, and (√2/4)K.

Findings (2)
The stated conditions do not make counterfactual fairness imply demographic parity. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 11, Section 5.2.1, relation to Equation (3)
Analysis
The paper permits predictors based on inferred background variables, but those variables may be associated with the protected attribute. Counterfactual invariance therefore does not imply equality between observational groups.
Evidence
Let U be Bernoulli(1/2), A=U, X=A, and Y-hat=U. The observed pair (X,A) uniquely determines U, and Y-hat uses only U. Intervening on A leaves U unchanged, so Y-hat(a,U)=Y-hat(a',U)=U and Equation (8) holds. Nevertheless, P(Y-hat=1|A=1)=1 while P(Y-hat=1|A=0)=0, violating demographic parity in Equation (3).
A counterfactually fair predictor built from non-descendants of A need not satisfy equalised odds or calibration, even when Y is independent of A. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 11, Section 5.2.3, Equalised Odds and Calibration
Analysis
Non-descendants of A can share latent causes with A and Y. Using such a variable preserves counterfactual fairness under interventions on A but can create the conditional dependencies forbidden by equalised odds and calibration.
Evidence
Let independent U_A,U_Y be Bernoulli(1/2), let U_Z be uniform on [0,1], and set A=U_A, Y=U_Y, and Z=1[U_Z<p(U_A,U_Y)], where p(0,0)=0.1, p(1,0)=0.8, p(0,1)=0.7, and p(1,1)=0.9. Set Y-hat=Z. There is no directed path between A and Y, and A and Y are independent. Z is not a descendant of A, and intervening on A does not change Z, so Y-hat is counterfactually fair. Equalised odds fails because P(Y-hat=0|A=0,Y=0)=0.9 but P(Y-hat=0|A=1,Y=0)=0.2. Calibration also fails because P(Y=1|A=0,Y-hat=1)=0.7/(0.1+0.7)=0.875, whereas P(Y=1|A=1,Y-hat=1)=0.9/(0.8+0.9)=9/17.
Paper details

Authors: Joshua R. Loftus, Chris Russell, Matt J. Kusner, Ricardo Silva

Two central implications claimed between counterfactual and statistical fairness are false under the paper's stated SCM assumptions.

Findings (2)
Excluding a non-redundant protected predictor does not necessarily increase both false negatives and false positives. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 6, Tradeoffs, p. 16
Analysis
Reduced overall classification accuracy only requires the total number of errors to increase; one error type may decrease while the other increases.
Evidence
Let S have two equally sized groups and no L. Suppose P(Y=1|S=0)=0.9 and P(Y=1|S=1)=0.4. The Bayes classifier using S predicts 1 for S=0 and 0 for S=1, yielding, per 1000 cases, 50 false positives and 200 false negatives, with accuracy 0.75. Omitting S gives P(Y=1)=0.65, so the Bayes classifier predicts 1 for everyone, yielding 350 false positives but zero false negatives, with lower accuracy 0.65. Thus S is non-redundantly associated with Y and its exclusion reduces accuracy, but false negatives decrease rather than increase.

Algorithmic decision making and the cost of fairness Computer Science Errors found mathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (2)
Proposition 3.3 incorrectly claims a bijection between detention cost and optimal detention rate. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3, Proposition 3.3 and its proof, especially Eq. (7)
Analysis
The stated positive-density assumption does not make the optimal detention rate strictly decrease with cost under fairness constraints. Consequently, the proof's assertion that c1<c2 implies f(c1)>f(c2) is false.
Evidence
Let groups A and B each have probability 1/2. Conditional on A, let P=p_{Y|X} be uniform on [0,1]; conditional on B, let P be uniform on [0,1/2]; and let Y|X be Bernoulli(P). The marginal density of P is 3/2 on [0,1/2] and 1/2 on (1/2,1], so it is positive throughout [0,1]. Under statistical parity, suppose each group has detention rate alpha. The optimal rule detains each group's highest-risk alpha fraction, yielding expected benefit 3alpha/4-3alpha^2/8 and utility (3/4-c)alpha-3alpha^2/8. For every c>=3/4, the unique optimum is alpha=0. Thus, for example, f(0.8)=f(0.9)=0, contradicting injectivity and therefore the claimed bijection.
The predictive-equality proof drops the probability of the modified group when computing the utility improvement. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Proof of Theorem 3.2, displayed calculation corresponding to source lines 513-519
Analysis

Evidence
Writing q=Pr(g(X)=a*), the construction changes decisions only in group a*, so E[d'(X)-d(X)]=q[beta_2-beta_1], not beta_2-beta_1 as displayed. The utility difference should therefore be (1-c)q[beta_2-beta_1]. The omitted factor does not change the positive sign when q>0, but the stated equality is false.
Paper details

Authors: Sam Corbett-Davies, Emma Pierson, Avi Feller, Sharad Goel, Aziz Huq

Figure 6 evaluates 100 selected officers and contains only gender, marital-status, and race groups. The accompanying text instead repeatedly describes a top-150 selection and reports age-group disparities, despite identifying only gender, race, and marital status as the audited attributes.
Evidence
Figure 6's predicted-positive counts sum to 100 within each attribute: gender 11+89=100; marital status 2+23+73=98, with omitted/missing categories; race 20+7+70=97, likewise with omitted/missing categories. Its caption explicitly says Precision@100. Nevertheless, the text says 'in the top 150' and reports 18–35 and 36–45 age results, although Figure 6 has no age rows. Those age numbers exactly repeat the criminal-justice results reported earlier in Section 4.1.
The criminal-justice case study incorrectly reports a fivefold selection disparity for the combined 18–35 age group. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
experimental-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Section 4.1, Figure 4 and page 11
Analysis
Recomputing predicted prevalence from Figure 4's counts gives neither the stated 0.06% rate nor the stated fivefold ratio for people aged 18–35.
Evidence
Figure 4 gives ages 18–25: size 137,432, PP=79; ages 26–35: size 129,377, PP=44; ages 36–45: size 74,784, PP=9. Thus the combined 18–35 rate is (79+44)/(137,432+129,377)=123/266,809=0.0461%, while the 36–45 rate is 9/74,784=0.0120%. Their ratio is 3.84, not 5. The stated approximately 0.06% and fivefold ratio apply only to ages 18–25, not ages 18–35 as claimed.

Findings (2)
Reported value, underestimation, and overestimation scores violate an exact identity implied by their definitions. (major, high confidence)
Tag
experimental-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Equations (6), (9), and (10); Tables 1 and 3
Analysis
For every item, value unfairness equals underestimation unfairness plus overestimation unfairness. Averaging preserves this identity, but the tables report incompatible values.
Evidence
Let a=E_g[y]-E_g[r] and b=E_not-g[y]-E_not-g[r]. Equations (9) and (10) give u=|(-a)+-(-b)+| and o=|(a)+-(b)+|. For all real a,b, |a-b|=u+o, so U_val=U_under+U_over. In Table 3's None row, 0.234 is reported instead of 0.107+0.153=0.260. In Table 1's Value row, 0.245 is reported instead of 0.063+0.199=0.262. These discrepancies greatly exceed three-decimal rounding. Even under the stated smoothing, the smoothed value term must be at least the sum of the two smoothed directional terms, whereas both tables show the opposite.
Averaging signed errors before applying absolute-value or directional operations makes three metrics fail to measure the error disparities claimed in the text. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3.3, Equations (8)-(10)
Analysis
The paper claims absolute unfairness is high when one group has large reconstruction error and the other has small error, and describes the directional metrics as measuring unequal underestimation and overestimation. Their formulas can return zero in exactly that situation because positive and negative individual errors cancel before the nonlinear operation.
Evidence
Take one observed item and two users per group, all with true rating 1. Give disadvantaged users predictions 2 and 0, and advantaged users predictions 1 and 1. The disadvantaged group's mean absolute error is 1, mean underestimation is 0.5, and mean overestimation is 0.5; all three quantities are 0 for the advantaged group. Nevertheless, both groups have mean prediction 1 and mean rating 1, so Equations (8), (9), and (10) all return zero. These predictions are permitted by the stated matrix-factorization model.

Maintaining cooperation in complex social dilemmas using deep reinforcement learning Computer Science Errors found logical-errormathematical-error criticalmajorminor
Review details
Findings (3)
The punishment-length inequality has its payoff difference reversed. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Section 3, punishment-length equation immediately before Theorem 1; Appendix 8.2
Analysis
The paper says the D phase must make the partner lose relative to cooperation, but its defining inequality instead requires the D phase to give the partner a higher value. The proof later uses the opposite ordering.
Evidence
The construction requires V2(s',D_kC,D_kC)-V2(s',C,C)>αd for d>0. A loss from punishment instead requires V2(s',C,C)-V2(s',D_kC,D_kC)>αd, which is the ordering used in Appendix 8.2. Under the stated premise that D has worse payoffs, the construction's left-hand side is negative for a sufficiently long punishment while αd is positive.
Theorem 1 does not follow even after correcting the punishment inequality's sign. (critical, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Theorem 1 and Appendix 8.2
Analysis
The algorithm permits any α>1 and selects punishment cost greater than αd, whereas the proof requires cost greater than d*/δ. These conditions are not equivalent when α≤1/δ.
Evidence
Consider the one-state repeated game with δ=0.5 and payoffs (r1,r2): CC=(1,1), CD=(-1,1.8), DC=(1.8,-1), DD=(0,0). Mutual C uniquely maximizes welfare; mutual D is an equilibrium and is πD-dominant because against D, choosing D gives 0 rather than -1. Here d*=0.8, VC=2, VD=0, so Theorem 1's assumption 2>d*/δ=1.6 holds. Choose the allowed α=1.1. Under the intended punishment inequality, k=1 qualifies because VC-V(D_1C)=2-(0+0.5·2)=1>αd=0.88. Nevertheless, cooperating forever gives player 2 value 2, while deviating once gives 1.8 at the deviation, 0 during punishment, and 0.5 thereafter, totaling 2.3. Thus deviation is profitable and the theorem's claimed best response is false.
Figure 4 incorrectly states that cooperation is dominant when the temptation payoff is 0.5. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Appendix 8.1, Figure 4 caption
Analysis
The paper's own payoff specification shows that cooperation is not dominant at that parameter value.
Evidence
The appendix sets mutual defection to 0, the exploited cooperator's payoff to -s, and s=1.5w. At w=0.5, cooperating against a defector yields -0.75, while defecting yields 0. Therefore cooperation is not a dominant stage-game action, contrary to the caption.

Outlining Traceability: A Principle for Operationalizing Accountability in Computing Systems Computer Science Errors found logical-error major
Review details
Findings (1)
The halting problem does not imply that external testing cannot be formally sound or that design disclosure is therefore necessary. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 3.1.1, “Testing,” p. 4
Analysis
The halting problem concerns deciding termination for arbitrary programs and inputs. It neither distinguishes external from developer testing nor becomes avoidable when design information is disclosed. Consequently, the stated necessity of disclosure does not follow from it.
Evidence
A deterministic program with Boolean input and output, such as f(false)=true and f(true)=false, can be exhaustively tested by an outsider on its entire input domain without receiving design information. That external test completely and soundly establishes its input-output behavior. Conversely, disclosing the source of an arbitrary program does not solve its halting problem. Both observations directly invalidate the paper’s derivation.
Paper details

Authors: Joshua A. Kroll

One demonstrable logical error appears in the argument connecting undecidability to mandatory design disclosure.

Analysis
The paper says attribute frequencies were compared between all male and female biographies using a chi-square test. Findings (1)
The paper incorrectly claims that an unbounded sum of nonzero event probabilities makes catastrophe certain. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3.1, Aerospace, p. 3
Analysis
Divergence of the sum of event probabilities does not by itself imply that a catastrophe occurs with probability one. The relevant Borel–Cantelli conclusion requires additional dependence assumptions, such as independence, which the paper does not state.
Evidence
Let every failure event E_n be the same event E with P(E)=10^-9. Then sum_n P(E_n) diverges, but P(at least one failure)=P(E)=10^-9, not 1. Thus the paper's stated premise is satisfied while its conclusion fails.

Findings (2)
Predictive parity and unequal prevalence do not necessarily preclude error-rate balance. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 2.3, equation (2.6) and the paragraph immediately following it (pages 6–7)
Analysis
The paper claims that unequal prevalence plus predictive parity forces unequal false-positive or false-negative rates, without excluding perfect prediction. Its own equation (2.6) permits the perfect-prediction case.
Evidence
Let S perfectly classify Y at the chosen threshold: S>s_HR exactly when Y=1. In both groups, PPV=1, FPR=0, and FNR=0, so predictive parity and error-rate balance both hold even if P(Y=1|R=b) differs from P(Y=1|R=w). Substituting PPV=1 into (2.6) also yields FPR=0 for every prevalence. Thus the stated universal incompatibility requires an additional non-perfect-prediction assumption.

Section 3, equation (3.1), Proposition 3.1, Corollaries 3.1–3.2, and Appendix A (pages 8 and 14)
Analysis
Equation (3.1) assigns t_min to high-risk defendants and t_max to low-risk defendants. Proposition 3.1 and its proof instead assign t_max to the high-risk event, reversing the policy and the sign of every resulting disparity formula.
Evidence
Take t_min=0, t_max=1, with every black defendant high-risk and every white defendant low-risk. Equation (3.1) gives E(T|R=b)=0 and E(T|R=w)=1, hence Delta=-1. Proposition 3.1 gives (1-0)(1-0)=+1. The two statements cannot both be true.

Authors: Alexandra Chouldechova

Two central mathematical claims fail as written: the fairness impossibility omits a perfect-prediction exception, and the defined MinMax policy contradicts its subsequent derivation.

Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample

Section 2.4, Proposition 1

Evidence

Findings (4)
Statistical parity does not imply the posterior group-membership bound in Proposition 3.1(2). (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type

Location
Page 7, Proposition 3.1(2)
Analysis
Equal outcome distributions conditional on group do not make posterior probabilities of membership in unequal-sized groups equal.
Evidence
Let S contain one person, T=S^c contain three, and sample uniformly from V. Let M always output a. Then D_tv(mu_S,mu_T)=0, so exact statistical parity holds. For O={a}, however, Pr{x in S | M(x) in O}=1/4 and Pr{x in T | M(x) in O}=3/4. Their difference is 1/2, violating the claimed bound with epsilon=0.
The exponential mechanism as defined is not (D_infinity,d)-Lipschitz. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 15, Definition 5.1 and Lemma 5.1
Analysis
Normalization can contribute additional privacy loss, so the exponent -d(x,y) does not ensure the claimed Lipschitz constant.
Evidence
Take V={x,y,z} with d(x,y)=d(x,z)=1 and d(y,z)=2, a valid metric. Then Z_x=1+2/e and Z_y=1+e^-1+e^-2. For outcome y, E(y)(y)/E(x)(y)=e Z_x/Z_y>e because Z_x>Z_y. Therefore D_infinity(E(x),E(y))>1=d(x,y), contradicting Lemma 5.1.

In a line metric, take centers y1=0 and y2=3k/4. They form a k/2-packing, yet the point 3k/8 lies in both B(y1,k/2) and B(y2,k/2). Hence those balls can overlap, invalidating the displayed equality and ensuing contradiction.

Findings (3)
The paper incorrectly interprets a ratio of regression slopes as the fraction of total variation occurring within scientists. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Supplementary Text, pp. 13–14, “Random effects versus fixed effects models” and “Controlling for topics in science”; main text p. 4
Analysis
The coefficients measure conditional changes in mean disruption per unit of team size. Their ratio is not a decomposition of outcome variation and cannot establish that two-thirds of total variation occurs within scientists. No variance components or sums of squares supporting that percentage are reported.
Evidence
The paper reports β1 = -0.00027 and β2 = -0.00018, then infers that two-thirds of total variation is within scientists because β2/β1 = 0.667. The same operation with β4/β1 = 0.00017/0.00027 = 0.630 is used for the topic-controlled claim. These calculations yield ratios of slopes only. Within-author team-size variance could approach zero while retaining the same within-author slope, making within-author explained variation approach zero without changing either reported ratio.
The expected reference-popularity formula is not the expectation of the stated finite-range power law and can become negative. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Figure S22 caption, pp. 7–8 of the supplement
Analysis
For p(k) proportional to k^-α on the stated interval from 1 to a finite maximum K, the expectation depends on K. The reported expression applies only to an unbounded continuous power law with α > 2.
Evidence
The paper states that integrating to maximum k gives E[k] = (α-1)/(α-2), with α = 2.4m^-0.05. At the explicitly analyzed team size m = 50, α ≈ 1.974 and the formula gives E[k] ≈ -36.9, impossible for citation counts k ≥ 1. With an infinite upper limit the expectation instead diverges when α ≤ 2; with a finite upper limit it remains positive and depends on K.

Findings (1)
The completion criterion does not imply the stated upper bound on average chain length. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Page 2, paragraph defining a searchable network, q = <(1-p)^L> >= r
Analysis
For 0 < 1-p < 1, the function f(L)=(1-p)^L is convex. Jensen's inequality therefore has the opposite useful direction from the paper's inference. No concentration or fixed-length assumption is stated that would justify replacing the expectation by the function of the mean.
Evidence
Jensen gives q=E[(1-p)^L] >= (1-p)^{E[L]}. Thus q>=r does not imply E[L]<=ln(r)/ln(1-p); distributions with a small probability of short chains and most probability on arbitrarily long chains can satisfy q>=r while having an arbitrarily large mean. Consequently, the asserted requirement E[L]<=10.4 for p=0.25 and r=0.05 does not follow.

Findings (1)
The vertex-move update uses the wrong sign for edges from the moved vertex to third groups. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section III, Eq. (25), pp. 5–6
Analysis
Moving vertex i from r to s removes its k_it edges from m_rt, so the updated value is m_rt-k_it. Equation (25) instead evaluates a(m_rt+k_it), and therefore does not equal the change in Eq. (23).
Evidence
Take vertices i,x in r, y in s, and z in t, with edges (i,z), (x,z), and (y,z). Moving i from r to s gives k_it=1, m_rt=2, m_st=1, κr=2, and κs=1. Afterward, m_rt=1, m_st=2, κr=1, and κs=2, merely exchanging the r and s statistics, so Eq. (23) has true change 0. Equation (25), however, gives a(3)-a(2)+a(2)-a(1)=6 log 3; its κ terms cancel and all other terms vanish. Thus it incorrectly reports a nonzero change. Replacing m_rt+k_it by m_rt-k_it fixes this case.

Findings (6)
Theorem 4 is false because the defined fluidity can be infinite for a network whose expected beliefs do not concentrate. (critical, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Definition 2, equations (40)-(41), and Theorem 4, equations (45)-(46)
Analysis
The logarithmic denominator in psi(P), combined with the supremum over admissible P, can approach zero while the numerator remains positive. This makes Psi infinite and forces Theorem 4's bound to zero even when agents have different expected beliefs.
Evidence
Take one regular agent a and two stubborn agents s0,s1 with equal-rate links a->s0 and a->s1, and beliefs 0 and 1. For r approaching e^2/8 from below, use the admissible transition matrix, in state order (a,s0,s1), P_r=[[(5-r)/5,r/10,r/10],[(1-r)/5,(4+r)/10,(4+r)/10],[(1-r)/5,(4+r)/10,(4+r)/10]]. Its stationary distribution is (1-r,r/2,r/2). For r near e^2/8, the maximal total-variation distance after k steps is r(4/5)^k, so tau=8. Thus taupi(S)=8r approaches e^2 from below and psi(P_r)=3(1-r)/[8r log(e^2/(8r))] diverges, giving Psi=infinity. Symmetry gives E[Z]=E[X_a]=1/2, while E[X_s0]=0 and E[X_s1]=1. With epsilon=1/4 and Delta=1, the left side of (45) is 2/3 but the right side is 0.

Analysis

Evidence
Use the tree s0-w1-w2-s1 with an additional leaf v attached to w1, and set x0=0,x1=1. Harmonicity gives E[X_v]=E[X_w1]=1/3. Equation (29), however, uses d(v,s0)=2 and d(v,s1)=3 and gives E[X_v]=2/5. For theta=1, the actual variance is (1/3)(2/3)=2/9, whereas the paper's distance formula gives 6/25.

Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample

Findings (6)
The two stated equivalent likelihoods for an undirected G(N,p) graph are not equivalent. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Section 3.2, page 22, G(N,p) likelihood formulas
Analysis
The adjacency matrix is symmetric, but the matrix likelihood multiplies over every ordered pair i≠j, counting each undirected edge twice. It therefore disagrees with the preceding likelihood.
Evidence
For N=2 and the graph containing its single possible edge, the first formula gives p, while the product over (1,2) and (2,1) gives p². Moreover, if the first formula truly denotes the probability of observing E edges, it omits the factor binom(binom(N,2),E).
The degree distribution of G(N,E) is incorrectly stated to be binomial. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.1, page 42
Analysis
With a fixed total number of edges, incident-edge indicators are dependent. A vertex degree is hypergeometric, not binomial.
Evidence
In G(3,2), a fixed vertex has degree 1 with probability 2/3 and degree 2 with probability 1/3, while degree 0 is impossible. The claimed binomial model Bin(2,2/3) instead assigns probabilities 1/9, 4/9, and 4/9 to degrees 0, 1, and 2.

Findings (2)
The stated CTRW mean-square-displacement exponent ν=2β/α is not an MSD exponent under either interpretation of the jump distribution. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Page 3, Scaling Anomalies (C), lines 99–104; related reasoning for Eq. (7) on pages 5–6
Analysis
For the untruncated stated tail P(Δr)∼Δr^(-1-α) with α<2, the second moment diverges, so the MSD is undefined. With the paper's stated cutoff, the second moment is finite, but independent zero-mean random-direction jumps give an MSD proportional to the expected number of jumps, not to its 2/α power. Thus ν=2β/α describes a typical stable-law displacement scale, not the claimed MSD.
Evidence
Let X(t)=Σ_{j=1}^{N(t)}J_j. With random directions, E[J_j]=0, so E[|X(t)|²|N]=N E[|J|²]. Under a cutoff, E|J|² is finite and therefore E|X(t)|²=E[N(t)]E|J|². If E[N(t)]∼t^β, the MSD exponent is β; after the waiting-time cutoff it is 1. Without the jump cutoff, E|J|²∝∫r²r^(-1-α)dr=∫r^(1-α)dr diverges for the paper's α=0.55. Neither case yields a finite MSD scaling as t^(2β/α).
The assertion that simple diffusive processes are not recurrent in two dimensions is false. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 3, Scaling Anomalies (C), lines 103–108
Analysis
The two-dimensional simple symmetric nearest-neighbor random walk is both diffusive and recurrent, directly contradicting the stated general claim.
Evidence
For the simple symmetric walk on Z², E|X_n|²=n, so it is diffusive. Its return probability after 2n steps is C(2n,n)²/4^(2n)∼1/(πn). The sum of these return probabilities diverges, which implies that the walk returns to its starting point with probability one. It is therefore a concrete recurrent two-dimensional simple diffusion.

The total attractiveness per vertex is m+k0. Stationary balance gives p_k/p_(k-1)=(k-1+k0)/(k+k0+(m+k0)/m), whose large-k expansion yields exponent alpha=2+k0/m, not 2+1/m. For example, k0=2 gives alpha=2+2/m.

Evidence type

Analysis
The section states that attachment probability is proportional to k+k0, but Equation (72) uses k in both influx and loss terms.
Evidence
The loss rate for degree-k vertices must be m(k+k0)p_k/(2m+k0), and at k=m it must be m(m+k0)p_m/(2m+k0). Equation (72) instead uses mkp_k/(2m+k0) and m^2p_m/(2m+k0), which are valid only for k0=0.

Evidence

Tag

Analysis

The asserted exponential distribution does not satisfy the functional equation used to derive it. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type

Location
Page 2, Section II, Boltzmann-Gibbs distribution
Analysis
The paper defines P as a normalized probability density and asserts P(m1+m2)=P(m1)P(m2), followed by the claimed solution P(m)=C exp(-m/T). An arbitrary prefactor C is incompatible with that equation.
Evidence
Substitution gives C exp(-(m1+m2)/T) on the left and C^2 exp(-(m1+m2)/T) on the right, requiring C=C^2. The subsequently derived C=1/T therefore fails for general T. The equation is also dimensionally inconsistent because a density is equated to a product of two densities.

A Convex Framework for Fair Regression Computer Science Errors found logical-errormathematical-error major
Review details
Findings (3)
A constant regressor minimizing each fairness penalty does not imply convergence to a constant as λ increases. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 2.2, page 4, “What minimizes these penalties?”
Analysis
Group fairness can be zero for nonconstant regressors. The accuracy-optimal zero-penalty model can therefore remain nonconstant for arbitrarily large λ.
Evidence
Let each group contain examples with labels +1 and -1, with features M e1, M e2 in group 1 and M e3, M e4 in group 2. Use d(y,y')=exp(-(y-y')²) and w=(1/M,-1/M,1/M,-1/M). Predictions equal the labels, so MSE=0. In Equation 2 the weighted cross-pair differences are 0, 2e^-4, -2e^-4, 0, whose sum is zero; hence f2(w)=0 although w is nonconstant. Its full objective is 2γ/M for every λ. Choosing M>2γ makes this less than 1, the minimum MSE of any constant predictor on these balanced labels. Thus no constant predictor can be optimal, even as λ→∞.
Jensen’s inequality does not establish that group fairness is always less costly than individual fairness under the allowed weights. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 4.1, page 7, paragraph beginning “Note that in theory”
Analysis
Jensen gives (mean(dΔ))² ≤ mean(d²Δ²), whereas Equation 1 is mean(dΔ²). The claimed comparison additionally requires d≤1, which the paper never assumes.
Evidence
Take one cross pair with equal labels, d(y,y')=2exp(-(y-y')²), and prediction difference Δ=1. This d is nonnegative and decreasing as required. Equation 1 gives f1=2, while Equation 2 gives f2=4, directly reversing the claimed ordering.

The definition divides by ℓP(w*), but no assumption requires this quantity to be positive. Consequently the subsequent claims PoF(α)≥1 and PoF(1)=1 do not hold throughout the stated setting.
Evidence
Let P place equal mass on (e1,1) and (e2,-1), assigned to the two groups, and use linear regression. The model w*=(1,-1) has MSE ℓP(w*)=0. At α=1, w* satisfies fP(w*)≤fP(w*), so the constrained numerator is also zero. The defined PoF(1) is therefore 0/0, not 1.
Paper details

Authors: Richard Berk, Hoda Heidari, Shahin Jabbari, Matthew Joseph, Michael Kearns, Jamie Morgenstern, Seth Neel, Aaron Roth

Three core mathematical claims fail under the paper’s stated assumptions.

Empirical observation of negligible fairness-accuracy trade-offs in machine learning for public policy Computer Science Errors found mathematical-error majorminor

Findings (2)
Monotonic recall does not guarantee an exactly balanced, unique allocation for a fixed integer top-k list. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 4, paragraph preceding “Measuring Fairness”; Methods, page 13; Supplementary Discussion, pages 1–3
Analysis
Recall at successive list depths is a discrete step function. Monotonicity alone guarantees neither existence nor uniqueness of an allocation having equal subgroup recalls. The paper nevertheless repeatedly claims that a unique recall-balancing solution exists, apart from score ties.
Evidence
Consider two groups with distinct scores and k=2. Group A has one positive ranked first and one negative ranked second, so its total positive count is 1. Group B has two positives ranked first and second, so its total positive count is 2. The possible prefix allocations (k_A,k_B) are (0,2), (1,1), and (2,0), producing recall pairs (0,1), (1,1/2), and (1,0), respectively. None achieves equal recall despite monotonic recall and no score ties.
Equation 6 does not imply that group-specific score scaling preserves within-group rankings. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Supplementary Discussion, Equations 3–6, especially the conclusion following Equation 6
Analysis
The derived score multiplies the original probability by 1+lambda_g/P[G=g,Y=1]. Being group-specific is insufficient to preserve ordering; the multiplier must also be positive. No such condition is stated or derived for the Lagrange parameter.
Evidence
Let P[G=g,Y=1]=0.2 and lambda_g=-0.3. The multiplier is 1-0.3/0.2=-0.5. For two same-group examples with original probabilities 0.8 and 0.2, Equation 6 gives scores -0.9 and -0.6, respectively. Their ordering is reversed, directly contradicting the claim that this transformation cannot reorder scores within a subgroup.
Paper details

Authors: Kit T. Rodolfa, Hemank Lamba, Rayid Ghani

The paper makes two mathematically false claims about its recall-balancing construction.

Findings (1)
Theorem 3 incorrectly estimates the structural-error variance using residuals formed with the estimated instruments rather than the endogenous regressors. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Theorem 3, page 18; Appendix B, Step 5, page 36
Analysis
The theorem defines the variance estimator as En[(y1i-Ahat_i' alphahat*)^2], but the structural equation is y1i=d_i'alpha+zeta_i. Accordingly, Appendix B proves consistency for residuals y1i-d_i'alphahat*, not for the estimator stated in the theorem. Replacing d_i by Ahat_i leaves first-stage noise in the residual and generally does not estimate sigma_zeta^2.
Evidence
Take no controls, alpha1=1, f(zi)=xi1 with normalized design, beta0=(1,0,...,0), and independent vi,zeta_i ~ N(0,1). Choose a growing design satisfying Condition SE and log^2(p or n)=o(n). Then all ASIV requirements hold, fhat converges to f, and alphahat* converges to 1. The theorem's residual converges to y1i-f(zi)=vi+zeta_i, so its empirical variance converges to 2, whereas sigma_zeta^2=1. By contrast, the Appendix B residual y1i-y2i alphahat* converges to zeta_i and has variance 1.

arXiv · PDF · 1806.05180v1

Reviewed 2026-07-26 06:44:29+00:00 UTC · gpt-5.6-sol (high)
The Frontiers of Fairness in Machine Learning Computer Science Errors found logical-error major
Review details
Findings (1)
Minimizing overall error need not fit the majority population when the groups cannot both be fit optimally. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 2, Section 2.1, item 2, “Minimizing Average Error Fits Majority Populations”
Analysis
Group prevalence determines the weights in overall error, but not which group-optimal classifier wins; the magnitudes of the competing error reductions also matter. A minority group can therefore determine the overall optimum despite being much smaller.
Evidence
Let the majority have probability 0.9 and the minority 0.1. For the majority, let (X,Y)=(0,0) with probability 0.99 and (1,0) with probability 0.01. For the minority, let (X,Y)=(1,1) always. Consider group-blind classifiers h0(x)=0 and h1(x)=x. No group-blind classifier can attain zero error in both groups because individuals with X=1 have conflicting labels across groups. h0 is majority-optimal, with majority error 0 and minority error 1, giving overall error 0.1. h1 is minority-optimal, with majority error 0.01 and minority error 0, giving overall error 0.9×0.01=0.009. Thus overall error minimization selects the minority-optimal classifier, and the minority has lower—not higher—error.
Paper details

Authors: Alexandra Chouldechova, Aaron Roth

A central claim about average-error minimization necessarily favoring the majority population is false.

On the (im)possibility of fairness Computer Science Errors found factual-errormathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (3)
Theorem 4.2 falsely claims that every rich mechanism into a discrete decision space is unfair. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Theorem 4.2 and proof, Section 4, page 11
Analysis
The theorem lacks assumptions ensuring that distinct construct-space points occur within the fairness threshold. Its proof also reverses the distortion inequality: observed distance at most delta implies construct distance at most delta plus epsilon, not delta minus epsilon.
Evidence
Let CS=OS={a,b} with d(a,b)=1, let the observation map be the identity, and let DS={0,1} have the discrete metric. Set epsilon=0.1, delta=0.5, and delta'=0.5. Define f(a)=0 and f(b)=1. The WYSIWYG distortion is 0<=0.1 and f is rich. For (delta-epsilon,delta')=(0.4,0.5)-fairness, every pair at construct distance at most 0.4 consists of the same point, so its output distance is 0<=0.5. Thus f is fair, contrary to Theorem 4.2.
The claimed lower bound rho_w>=delta from random radius-delta perturbation is false. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Definition 3.4 discussion, page 7; Theorem 4.3 proof, page 12
Analysis
Perturbing points within radius delta does not force any positive change, much less a deterministic lower bound of delta. Therefore the denominator bound used to derive the nondiscrimination guarantee does not follow.
Evidence
Take X and Y to be identical two-point discrete metric spaces, partitioned into corresponding singleton groups, and choose 0<delta<1. Every radius-delta ball is a singleton, so the prescribed perturbation leaves every point unchanged. Each corresponding singleton pair has Gromov-Wasserstein distance zero, hence rho_w=0, not rho_w>=delta. The group-skew ratio remains undefined, directly refuting the earlier assertion that this perturbation always makes it well-defined and invalidating the denominator step in Theorem 4.3.
The displayed four-fifths ratio uses a negative-outcome probability while the text defines it as a ratio of positive-outcome probabilities. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
factual-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Section 5.2, page 14
Analysis
The prose and formula define different quantities and generally cannot both describe the stated measure.
Evidence
The text says the denominator is the majority group's probability of a positive classification, Pr[C=YES|G=1], but displays Pr[C=YES|G=0]/Pr[C=0|G=1]. For example, if the protected positive rate is 0.4 and the majority positive rate is 0.8, the stated positive-rate ratio is 0.5 while the displayed formula gives 0.4/0.2=2.
Paper details

Authors: Sorelle A. Friedler, Carlos Scheidegger, Suresh Venkatasubramanian

Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 5.2, discussion following Table 4
Analysis
False positives can reduce precision and consequently F1, but they do not enter recall. Recall equals TP/(TP+FN), so low recall must result from false negatives relative to true positives. Therefore the stated inference from many false positives to unsatisfactory recall does not follow.
Evidence
The paper states that models “generate many fake positive cases. Thus, the recall and F1 values are far from being satisfactory.” Holding TP and FN fixed while adding any number of false positives leaves recall unchanged.

Findings (3)
Peterson and Novick's conditional-probability criterion is incorrectly called sufficiency. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Section 3.1, “Equivalent Notions,” first bullet; Table 3
Analysis
The criterion is A ⊥ D | Y, which the paper elsewhere identifies as separation, while sufficiency reverses the conditioned variables to A ⊥ Y | D.
Evidence
Section 3.1 calls A ⊥ D | Y both “sufficiency” and “equalized odds.” Table 3 instead labels the same Peterson–Novick criterion “separation,” and Section 4.3 explicitly distinguishes separation A ⊥ R | Y from sufficiency A ⊥ Y | R. Both classifications cannot be correct.
Criterion (3) does not make errors from predicting Y using R uncorrelated with A. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 2.2, discussion following Darlington's four criteria
Analysis
For standardized variables, the least-squares predictor is Ŷ=ρ_RY R, so Cov(A,Y−Ŷ)=ρ_AY−ρ_ARρ_RY. Zero error correlation therefore gives criterion (1), ρ_AR=ρ_AY/ρ_RY, not criterion (3), ρ_AR=ρ_AYρ_RY.
Evidence
Take a valid correlation matrix with ρ_AY=0.2, ρ_RY=0.5, and criterion-(3) value ρ_AR=0.1; its determinant is 0.72. Then Cov(A,Y−Ŷ)=0.2−0.1×0.5=0.15, not zero.
Darlington's criterion (2) is not equivalent to independence even under a multivariate Gaussian distribution. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3.1, “Equivalent Notions,” sixth bullet
Analysis
For jointly Gaussian A and R, independence requires ρ_AR=0. Criterion (2) instead requires ρ_AR=ρ_AY, which need not vanish. Table 3 also contradicts the prose by assigning criterion (2) no modern independence counterpart.
Evidence
A positive-definite Gaussian correlation matrix can have ρ_AR=ρ_AY=0.2 and ρ_RY=0.5; its determinant is 0.71. Criterion (2) holds, but A and R are dependent because ρ_AR≠0.
Paper details

Authors: Ben Hutchinson, Margaret Mitchell

Three demonstrable errors occur in the paper's central mapping of correlation-based criteria to modern fairness notions.

Preventing Fairness Gerrymandering: Auditing and Learning for Subgroup Fairness Computer Science Errors found mathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (3)
Claim C.1 incorrectly loses a factor of two when transferring an Auditor best response between nearby distributions. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Appendix C, Claim C.1 and its use in Lemma 4.12, pages 32–33
Analysis
Uniform payoff error xi only guarantees a 2xi-approximate best response, not the claimed xi guarantee. The false claim is used directly in the Auditor-regret bound supporting Theorem 4.2.
Evidence
Take two negative examples, group g selecting the first, C=1, and gamma=0.05. Let hA=(1,0) and hB=(0,1). Then err(hA)=err(hB)=0.5; for hA, Phi-minus=0.20 and Phi-plus=-0.30; for hB these values are reversed. Let D put all mass on hA and let D-hat put masses 0.25 and 0.75 on hA and hB. For every lambda in Lambda, the payoff difference between D and D-hat is at most xi=0.375. D-hat's unique best response is lambda-plus, whose payoff under D is 0.20. But D's maximum payoff is 0.70 at lambda-minus, so Claim C.1 requires 0.70-0.375 <= 0.20, i.e. 0.325 <= 0.20, which is false.
Algorithm 2's stated sample-size formula can prescribe zero samples on an allowed problem instance, leaving its empirical distributions undefined. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Algorithm 2 (FairNR), page 19
Analysis
The sample size multiplies by d2 log n rather than retaining the positive log-confidence term additively. Consequently, the algorithm is not defined for valid group classes of VC dimension zero.
Evidence
Let n=2 and G contain only the constant-one group, so d2=VCDIM(G)=0; let both examples be negative and let H contain the constant classifiers. CSC oracles are trivial and all stated assumptions hold. Algorithm 2 nevertheless sets m proportional to d2 log n, hence m=0. It then defines D-hat-t and its final output as empirical distributions over zero draws, which do not exist.
Appendix B bounds only the conjunction probability and therefore does not prove SP uniform convergence as claimed. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Theorem 2.11 and Appendix B, page 28
Analysis
The fairness quantity is |P(g)P(h)-P(hg)|. The proof controls only |P(hg)-P_S(hg)| and immediately concludes the theorem, without controlling the separate product P(g)P(h). That controlled term alone cannot bound the theorem's left-hand side.
Evidence
Let the population assign probability 1/4 to each of the four (h,g) label pairs. Then P(h)=P(g)=1/2, P(hg)=1/4, and the SP fairness quantity is zero. An empirical distribution with masses 1/4, 1/2, 1/4, and 0 on (1,1),(1,0),(0,1),(0,0) has P_S(hg)=1/4, so the sole term bounded in Appendix B is zero, while its fairness quantity is |(3/4)(1/2)-1/4|=1/8. Thus the asserted conclusion does not follow from the displayed bound.
Paper details

Authors: Michael Kearns, Seth Neel, Aaron Roth, Zhiwei Steven Wu

Three demonstrable mathematical defects occur in the generalization and no-regret analyses.

Findings (2)
The defined polysemy score cannot be logarithmically transformed or raised to the reported fractional powers. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 4, Equations (7), (10), and (11)
Analysis
Equation (10) defines d(w_i) as the negative of a nonnegative clustering coefficient, so d(w_i) is nonpositive. Equation (7) nevertheless uses log(d(w_i)), and Equation (11) raises d(w_i) to generally noninteger exponents between 0.08 and 0.53. Both operations are undefined over the reals for negative d, and log(0) is also undefined.
Evidence
The numerator and denominator in Equation (10) are nonnegative, with a leading minus sign; hence d(w_i)≤0. But the model requires log(d(w_i)), and the claimed law requires d(w_i)^βd for βd∈[0.08,0.53]. Thus the stated definition cannot produce the real-valued regression and power law reported.
The regression coefficients are incorrectly identified as power-law exponents after standardizing the log-displacement outcome. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 4.1 and Equations (7)–(9)
Analysis
The paper says that log displacement is normalized to zero mean and unit variance before regression. A coefficient on log frequency in this standardized-outcome model is not directly the exponent of the unstandardized displacement power law.
Evidence
Let Y=log Δ and let the reported normalized outcome be Ỹ=(Y−μ)/s. If Equation (7) gives Ỹ=βf log f+βd log d+…, then Y=μ+sβf log f+sβd log d+…, so Δ∝f^(sβf)d^(sβd). Equations (8) and (9) instead use βf and βd themselves as the exponents, omitting the factor s. The reported ranges therefore cannot be interpreted as the claimed power-law exponents without undoing the normalization.

Fairness Constraints: Mechanisms for Fair Classification Statistics Errors found experimental-errorlogical-errormathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (5)
The stated nonlinear-SVM dual has the trivial zero classifier as its optimizer. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix A, Equation (10)
Analysis
The objective has the wrong sign structure for an SVM dual. Under the paper's own kernel definition, every term in the objective is nonnegative, while the constraints permit alpha=0.
Evidence
Expanding the objective gives sum_i alpha_i + alpha^T YKY alpha + sum_i alpha_i^2/C. The paper defines K as a Gram matrix, so alpha^T YKY alpha is nonnegative; alpha_i>=0 and C>0 make the other terms nonnegative. Thus alpha=0 attains objective zero and every nonzero feasible alpha has strictly positive objective. Equation (10) therefore always returns the zero decision function, independently of the training data.
Satisfying the 100%-rule does not imply approximately zero decision-boundary covariance. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3.1, Equations (2)-(3)
Analysis
Equal positive-class rates constrain only the signs of distances, whereas covariance also depends on their magnitudes.
Evidence
Take equally sized groups with z=0 distances {-1,1} and z=1 distances {-1,100}. Both groups have positive-class probability 1/2, satisfying the 100%-rule exactly. Yet z-bar=1/2 and the empirical covariance is [(-1/2)(-1)+(-1/2)(1)+(1/2)(-1)+(1/2)(100)]/4=12.375, not zero. Replicating these observations produces arbitrarily large training sets with the same nonzero covariance.
The fine-grained logistic-loss constraint does not guarantee that an initially positive prediction remains positive. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3.3, Equation (8) and the paragraph immediately following it
Analysis
The individual loss rewards probability assigned to the true label, not necessarily to the positive label. The claimed implication fails for false-positive predictions.
Evidence
Let y_i=-1 while the unconstrained classifier predicts positive with p(+1)=0.6 and p(-1)=0.4. With gamma_i=0, Equation (8) requires -log p_new(-1)<=-log(0.4), or p_new(-1)>=0.4. A new classifier with p_new(-1)=0.9 and p_new(+1)=0.1 satisfies the constraint but changes the prediction from positive to negative.
A decreasing CV score does not generally correspond to an increasing p%-rule. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Appendix B.2, final paragraph
Analysis
The CV score is an absolute difference, while the p%-rule is a ratio; their orderings are not equivalent.
Evidence
Positive rates (0.01,0.02) give CV=0.01 and p%-rule=50%. Rates (0.40,0.45) give CV=0.05 and p%-rule=88.9%. Thus the smaller CV score can have the smaller, rather than larger, p%-rule.
One Gaussian distribution used for the nonlinear synthetic experiment has an impossible covariance matrix. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
experimental-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Appendix B.1, nonlinear synthetic-data definition
Analysis
A covariance matrix must be symmetric, but the stated matrix is not.
Evidence
The paper specifies N([4;-4],[[4,4],[2,5]]). Its off-diagonal entries are 4 and 2, contradicting Cov(X1,X2)=Cov(X2,X1). Consequently, the stated Gaussian distribution is undefined.

Authors: Muhammad Bilal Zafar, Isabel Valera, Manuel Gomez Rodriguez, Krishna P. Gummadi

Five demonstrable internal errors affect the nonlinear-SVM formulation and several claimed fairness guarantees.

An Introduction to Collective Intelligence Computer Science Errors found mathematical-error majorminor

Findings (4)
Theorem 2(ii)'s guarantee that effect-set WLU is factored fails for permitted discrete states. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.3.3, Theorem 2(ii), pages 53–54
Analysis
The effect set is defined using nonzero derivatives, but the paper explicitly permits discrete unary states evaluated at hypercube vertices. A finite state change can affect a future component even when its derivative vanishes at every allowed vertex, causing WLU to omit an actual effect.
Evidence
Let one node have allowed state x in {0,1} at time 0 and deterministic future state y=h(x)=3x^2-2x^3. This is a smooth Euclidean extension with h(0)=0, h(1)=1, but h'(0)=h'(1)=0. Thus the effect set contains x itself but omits y. Set G(x,y)=y. Clamping the stated effect set changes x to 0 but leaves y unchanged, so WLU=G(x,y)-G(0,y)=0 for both allowed worldlines. Moving from x=0 to x=1 leaves WLU unchanged while G rises from 0 to 1, violating the sign equality defining factoredness in Equation (4).
A Nash equilibrium of a factored system need not be a local maximum of world utility. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.3.2, immediately after Equation (5), page 50
Analysis
Factoredness constrains unilateral changes only, whereas an ordinary local maximum must also resist simultaneous nearby changes by multiple nodes.
Evidence
Take two real-valued nodes with identity dynamics and set g1=g2=G=xy. This team game is factored by the paper's definition. At (x,y)=(0,0), either node's utility remains zero under every unilateral deviation, so (0,0) is a Nash equilibrium. Nevertheless, every neighborhood contains (epsilon,epsilon), where G=epsilon^2>0=G(0,0). Therefore (0,0) is not a local maximum of G.
Theorem 1's positive-derivative characterization excludes valid factored utilities. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.3.2, Theorem 1 and Equation (6), pages 51–52
Analysis
Strict preservation of utility ordering does not imply that the representing function has a strictly positive derivative at every value, and the proof never establishes that condition.
Evidence
Consider one real-valued node with identity dynamics, G(x)=x, and g(x)=x^3. Since x cubed is strictly increasing, sign(g(x)-g(x')) equals sign(G(x)-G(x')) for every pair, so the system is factored. Because G ranges over all real numbers, Equation (6) forces Phi(G)=G^3. But its derivative at G=0 is zero, contradicting Theorem 1's requirement that the derivative be greater than zero at every associated G value.

Evidence

Findings (1)
Unequal base rates do not make accuracy equity mathematically incompatible with equal false-positive rates. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 8, Section 3.3, final paragraph
Analysis
The paper defines accuracy equity as equal overall accuracy across groups and then claims that unequal base rates make it mathematically impossible to achieve alongside equalized false-positive rates. These conditions can coexist because true-positive rates may differ between groups.
Evidence
Consider 100 subjects per group. Group A has 20 positives and 80 negatives: TP=10, FN=10, FP=8, TN=72. Group B has 80 positives and 20 negatives: TP=64, FN=16, FP=2, TN=18. The base rates differ (20% versus 80%), but both false-positive rates equal 10% (8/80 and 2/20), and both accuracies equal 82% ((10+72)/100 and (64+18)/100). This directly violates the asserted impossibility.
Paper details

Authors: Reuben Binns

One explicit mathematical impossibility claim is disproved by a concrete counterexample.

Findings (4)
Theorem 4 applies a sparse-recovery bound to the wrong vector. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 4.3, Algorithm 2, Theorem 4, and supplementary equations (27) and (33)
Analysis
Algorithm 2 minimizes the l1 norm of the centered vector Δs and reconstructs s as the uniform vector plus Δs. A compressive-sensing guarantee for this optimization therefore depends on the best K-term approximation error of Δs, not of s. The proof substitutes σ_K(s) without establishing any relationship between the two quantities.
Evidence
Let N>=3, K=1, and s=(1,0,...,0), which is the Shapley vector of the monotone utility U(S)=1 when player 1 is present and 0 otherwise. Then σ_1(s)=0. But the algorithm centers at bar{s}=1/N, so Δs=((N-1)/N,-1/N,...,-1/N), for which σ_1(Δs)=(N-1)/N>0. Thus the σ_K(s) term in equation (33) cannot follow from sparse recovery of Δs.
Theorem 6 gives a false necessary condition for additivity of the largest-subset attribution scheme. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.5, Theorem 6 and its supplementary proof
Analysis
The proof asserts that additivity holds if and only if the three normalization constants are equal. This fails when the two marginal-contribution vectors are linearly dependent.
Evidence
Take I={1,2}. Define U(empty)=0, U({1})=U({2})=1, U(I)=2, and V(empty)=0, V({1})=V({2})=2, V(I)=3. The largest-subset marginal vector is (1,1) for both utilities. Hence hat{s}(U)=(1,1), hat{s}(V)=(1.5,1.5), and hat{s}(U+V)=(2.5,2.5), so additivity holds. But the theorem's required equality fails: V(I)sum_i ΔU_i=32=6, whereas U(I)sum_i ΔV_i=22=4.
The stated fairness/null-player property is not satisfied by the Shapley value. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3, Fairness property (2) and the subsequent uniqueness claim
Analysis
The paper states that s_i=0 whenever U(S union {i})=0 for every S excluding i, then claims that the Shapley value uniquely satisfies the listed properties. The stated condition does not imply zero marginal contribution because U(S) may be nonzero.
Evidence
Let I={1,2}, U(empty)=0, U({1})=0, U({2})=1, and U({1,2})=0. For player 1, U(S union {1})=0 for both eligible subsets S=empty and S={2}, satisfying the paper's condition. Equation (1), however, gives s_1=(1/2)(0-0)+(1/2)(0-1)=-1/2, not zero.
The stratified Shapley decomposition has an off-by-one index and includes an impossible subset size. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Section 4.5, formula s_i=(1/N) sum from k=1 to N of E[X_i^k]
Analysis
Equation (1) averages marginal contributions over subsets of the other N-1 users, whose sizes range from 0 through N-1. Section 4.5 instead sums from 1 through N while defining X_i^k using a size-k subset sampled with probability 1/binomial(N-1,k).
Evidence
For N=2, the Section 4.5 formula requires a size-2 subset of the single user other than i and divides by binomial(1,2)=0, while omitting the empty-subset contribution. Equation (1) instead gives the two valid strata k=0 and k=1.

Findings (1)
The expected-cost formula uses mismatched attentional-state indices. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Page 307, Section 3, Equation (1)
Analysis
The paper sums over attentional states Fj but writes the cost as C0(Ai,Fi), which is independent of the summation index j. Consequently, the expression cannot average state-dependent interruption costs as claimed.
Evidence
As written, ECA = sum_j C0(Ai,Fi)p(Fj|Ea) = C0(Ai,Fi) sum_j p(Fj|Ea) = C0(Ai,Fi). Since the probabilities sum to one, uncertainty over attentional state has no effect. The required state-dependent expectation would instead use C0(Ai,Fj) inside the sum.

Evidence

Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location

Analysis
The displayed equation omits group probabilities and has the wrong coefficient on the budget, so the zero set used in the subsequent Sard-theorem argument is not the required set of budget-exhausting policies.
Evidence
Writing Fi(ti)=Pr(r(X)<ti|A=ai) and pi=Pr(A=ai), a threshold policy has E[D]=1−p0F0(t0)−p1F1(t1). Budget exhaustion therefore requires p0F0(t0)+p1F1(t1)=1−b. The paper instead asserts F0(t0)+F1(t1)=2−b. Even for equal group probabilities, the correct equation is F0+F1=2−2b.

Corollary 70 does not follow from Theorem 17 by embedding Y as Y(0)=Y(1). (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix F.5, Corollary 70, pp. 87–88
Analysis
Prevalence or shyness in an ambient distribution space is not generally preserved when pulled back to a lower-dimensional embedded subspace. The identity of the composed maps establishes only a set-theoretic correspondence, not the required genericity result.
Evidence
In finite dimensions, the x-axis is Lebesgue-null in R², but its preimage under the embedding x↦(x,0) is all of R. Thus an ambient null or shy exceptional set may contain the entire embedded subspace. The paper supplies no argument excluding this possibility for distributions satisfying Y(0)=Y(1).

Findings (2)
Theorem 3.2(b)'s derivative decomposition does not follow from its assumptions. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 10, Theorem 3.2(b)
Analysis
Assumption 4(a) requires differentiability only of the observed conditional mean. It does not impose the joint regularity needed to differentiate the treatment-effect function along its diagonal. The stated equality can therefore fail even when every derivative appearing in it exists.
Evidence
Let c=3/2, let D have positive mass at zero and otherwise be uniform on (1,2), and set all period-one outcomes to zero. Define H(u,v)=u^3/(u^2+v^2), with H(0,0)=0, and Y2(a)=H(a-c,D-c)-H(-c,D-c). Then Y2(0)=0, so PT holds. The observed mean is m(d)=(d-c)/2+c^3/(c^2+(d-c)^2), which is differentiable as required. At d=c, m'(c)=1/2, while ACRT(c|c)=1 and the stated selection derivative is 0. Theorem 3.2(b) therefore asserts 1/2=1.
The discrete-treatment ACRT estimator incorrectly subtracts the regression intercept for the first positive dose. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Pages 18-20, Equations (4.1) and (4.7)
Analysis
In regression (4.1), beta_0 is the untreated group's mean outcome change, whereas beta_j is the difference from that mean and hence estimates ATT(d_j). Thus ACRT(d_1) is beta_1/(d_1-d_0), not (beta_1-beta_0)/(d_1-d_0). The same error enters Equation (4.7).
Evidence
Take D in {0,1,2} with positive probabilities, Y1=0, and Y2(d)=5+d for every unit. SPT holds. Regression (4.1) gives beta_0=5, beta_1=1, and beta_2=2. The true ACRT(1) is (Y2(1)-Y2(0))/(1-0)=1, but the paper's formula gives (beta_1-beta_0)/(1-0)=-4.
