Findings (3)
The reported d'=2.45 and bias=0.52 are not validly estimable from the described stratified sample. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 3, Signal detection analysis of the robot’s accuracy
Analysis
The authors sampled 100 articles called OA and 100 called NOA, thereby conditioning on the robot's classification. Such a sample estimates predictive values, not the hit and false-alarm rates conditional on true status that d' requires. Treating its cross-tabulated counts as an unstratified confusion matrix is invalid, especially when the paper states that the population classes occur in roughly a 10:1 ratio.
Evidence
The called-OA sample contains 81 hits and 19 false alarms; the called-NOA sample contains 94 correct rejections and 6 misses. The reported d' results from using 81/(81+6) as the hit rate and 19/(19+94) as the false-alarm rate, but the equal sampling of predicted classes changes both denominators. Population sampling weights are required before those rates can be computed.

Tag
logical-error
Evidence type

Analysis

Evidence

Analysis

Evidence
Let d_theta(x)=x and let all labels be y=-1. For z=0 use x={M,-1}; for z=1 use x={1,-1}. Each group has FPR 1/2, while Eq. (11) gives g={-M,0} and {-1,0}. With z-bar=1/2, Cov(z,g)=(M-1)/8, arbitrarily large as M grows, contradicting the claim after Eq. (12). Conversely, use z=0: x={2,-1} and z=1: x={1,1}. Equation 13 is not an F statistic with the claimed null distribution. (major, high confidence)
Tag
statistical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.1.5, Equation 13, page 14
Analysis
The denominator sums residual magnitudes rather than squared magnitudes, while the numerator is squared. Consequently the statistic is not scale invariant and cannot have the stated variance-independent F distribution.
Evidence
Under the null, multiply every tapered transform by any c > 0. Equation 12 makes mu-hat scale by c. Equation 13's numerator then scales by c^2, but its denominator scales by c, so F scales by c. The stated AR coefficients define an unstable causal recursion, not the stationary background whose spectrum is plotted. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 4.1.3 and Figures 2-3, pages 6-8
Analysis
A causal AR process requires every zero of 1-sum(a_k z^k) to lie outside the unit circle. The paper's coefficients violate this condition.
Evidence
The polynomial is A(z)=1+0.683z-1.55z^2+1.96z^3-1.87z^4. A(-0.4)=0.305488 and A(-0.5)=-0.090875, so it has a root between -0.5 and -0.4, strictly inside the unit circle. The recursion therefore contains an explosive mode and does not support the causal stationary theoretical spectrum used in Figure 3.

Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 4.2.1, Equations 17-19, page 16
Analysis
The density defined in Equation 17 must scale inversely with singular-value units. Equation 18 lacks one factor of sigma in its denominator.
Evidence
Scale the noise matrix by c. Then sigma and every lambda scale by c, while the definition gives rho_c(c lambda)=rho(lambda)/c because delta(c x)=delta(x)/c. Equation 18 instead gives rho_c(c lambda)=rho(lambda): its square root and denominator both scale as c^2. Its integral therefore also fails to remain the fixed number of singular values.

Evidence
Data set B lasts 23 s. A full bandwidth of 0.3 Hz means W=0.15 Hz, giving 2TW=2(23)(0.15)=6.9. The stated rule therefore gives approximately 6-7 concentrated DPSS, not 13. Thirteen tapers would instead correspond to a half-bandwidth near 0.3 Hz, or a full bandwidth near 0.6 Hz.

mathematical-error

Findings (2)
Vanishing torsion does not imply that the two complex structures are equal. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Appendix, page 18, discussion preceding the Kähler quotient
Analysis
The paper states that if the torsion vanishes, then J+ = J−. This does not follow from the stated conditions that both structures are metric-compatible and preserved by the corresponding connections.
Evidence
Take Euclidean R4 with g = I4 and T = 0, so both connections equal the flat Levi-Civita connection. Let J be the standard constant complex structure on R2, and define J+ = diag(J,J) and J− = diag(J,−J). Both square to −I, are compatible with g, are integrable, and are covariantly constant, satisfying all conditions stated by the paper. Nevertheless J+ ≠ J−.
Applying equation (2.7) to the chiral-current action (3.3) does not return identical metric and b-field tensors as asserted in equation (3.4). (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3.1, equations (3.3)–(3.4), using equation (2.7)
Analysis
The off-diagonal metric and b-field components change sign. The resulting model is equivalent only after reflecting the dual coordinate, not tensor-by-tensor identical as equation (3.4) claims.
Evidence
For (3.3), E=g+b has E00=1, E0a=0, and Ea0=Ga, hence g0a=Ga/2 and b0a=−Ga/2. Equation (2.7) gives g̃0a=b0a=−Ga/2 and b̃0a=g0a=Ga/2. Thus g̃0a≠g0a and b̃0a≠b0a whenever Ga≠0. A case within the nonzero-torsion branch is Gx=0, Gy=x, for which dG≠0. Reflecting θ̃ restores equivalence, but does not validate the exact equality in (3.4).

Findings (1)
The reduction of the Bondi mass from Eq. (26) to Eq. (29) does not follow from localizing a wave packet in the Liouville region. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Pages 8–9, Eqs. (25)–(29)
Analysis
Equation (26) must be evaluated at future right null infinity, where Eq. (25) gives e^{-2φ}=e^{λ(σ^+−σ^-)}→∞, not in the small-e^{-2φ} Liouville region. Therefore, neglecting the dilaton action near the packet does not justify deleting the dilaton boundary term in the Bondi mass. The paper provides no constraint showing that this asymptotic term vanishes. Its subsequent claims that all Liouville-region excitations have zero energy and hence that all incoming energy must radiate away depend on this invalid reduction.
Evidence
Let D=σ^+−σ^- and take asymptotically vanishing deviations δρ=δφ=−(C/2)e^{-λD}. These obey the stated boundary condition δρ,δφ→0, but the first term of Eq. (26) tends 2e^{λD}[λδρ+∂+δφ−∂-δφ]=λC, while the N-dependent term tends zero. This is not artificial: transforming the paper's post-shock solution (13) gives δρ=δφ=−(1/2)ln(1+Ce^{-λD}), with precisely this leading behavior and a finite mass. Thus asymptotic vanishing does not permit Eq. (26)'s dilaton contribution to be discarded.

Findings (1)
The derived suppression factor applies only to radial Higgs fluctuations, not every off-shell Higgs mode as assumed in the RG equations. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 4, equations (25)-(26); Appendix A, especially equation (33)
Analysis
Equation (26) is derived after replacing the Higgs doublet by one real radial field. Extending the paper's own Einstein-frame kinetic formula to the original four real Higgs components shows that only the radial component receives the gravity-induced kinetic term. The three transverse components therefore have unsuppressed commutators. Applying s to every Higgs propagator, including suppressing the complete 24 lambda-squared contribution in equation (33), does not follow from the derivation.
Evidence
Write the doublet as four real fields h_a with f=1+xi sum(h_a^2)/m_Pl^2 and choose the background h_a=(phi,0,0,0). Equation (4) generalizes to G_ab=delta_ab/f+(3/2)m_Pl^2 f_,a f_,b/f^2. For each transverse direction a=2,3,4, f_,a=0 on this background, so G_aa=1/f. Using the paper's equation (26) construction gives s_a=f^(-1)G_aa^(-1)=1, even when phi is much larger than m_Pl/sqrt(xi). Only the radial mode has s=1/(6xi+1). Thus a transverse Higgs line is a concrete counter-instance to the claimed universal suppression, invalidating the stated modification of the full Standard Model beta functions.
Paper details

Authors: Andrea De Simone, Mark P. Hertzberg, Frank Wilczek

The loop-suppression prescription is incorrectly generalized from one radial Higgs mode to the full Higgs multiplet.

Universal and non-universal properties of cross-correlations in financial time series Physics and Astronomy Errors found mathematical-error major

Findings (1)
A small correlation-matrix eigenvalue does not imply that the contributing companies are uncorrelated. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Page 5, paragraph discussing IPR values near λ≈0.25; Figure 1 caption
Analysis
The claimed inference is opposite to the behavior of a correlation matrix. Mutually uncorrelated standardized variables have an identity correlation matrix and eigenvalues equal to 1. Moreover, the paper's own uncorrelated benchmark places eigenvalues in [0.37,1.94], so λ≈0.25 is a deviation from that benchmark, not evidence for uncorrelated companies.
Evidence
Consider two standardized companies with correlation matrix [[1,0.75],[0.75,1]]. Its localized eigenvector (1,-1)/sqrt(2) has eigenvalue 0.25 and IPR 1/2, while the companies are strongly correlated. Conversely, setting their correlation to zero gives the identity matrix and eigenvalue 1. Thus the observed combination of a localized vector and λ≈0.25 cannot support the paper's stated inference of mutual non-correlation.

Findings (2)
The proof of Theorem 1 does not establish optimality when the penalty sequence is bounded. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix A.3, equations (36)-(38); Theorem 1
Analysis
The theorem states no assumption that mu_k tends to infinity, and the paper emphasizes that ALM can converge without this. Nevertheless, the proof obtains an O(1/mu_k) error and a residual equal to (Y*_{k+1}-Y*_k)/mu_k, then declares both vanish by letting k tend to infinity and using boundedness of Y*k. Neither conclusion follows for bounded mu_k.
Evidence
If mu_k is bounded, O(1/mu_k) need not approach zero. Likewise, boundedness of a sequence does not imply Y*{k+1}-Y*_k tends to zero; for example, Y*_k=(-1)^k is bounded but has nonvanishing successive differences. Thus equations (37) and (38) require an additional argument or the missing assumption mu_k→infinity.
The practical penalty update is not consistent with Theorem 2 and can accept a grossly nonoptimal iterate. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Algorithm 5; Section 4, stopping criterion (22), update rule (25), and stated parameters
Analysis
Rule (25) can increase mu geometrically forever, making the reciprocal sum finite despite Theorem 2 requiring it to diverge. The paper's stopping test can then declare convergence at a nonoptimal feasible decomposition.
Evidence
Let m=n=2, lambda=1/sqrt(2), and D=M e_11 with M=100000. Using the stated mu_0=1.25/M gives Y_0=lambda e_11. Write c=1-lambda. The first IALM iteration yields A_1=(M-c/mu_0)e_11 and E_1=(c/mu_0)e_11, so A_1+E_1=D exactly. Its second stopping residual is mu_0||E_1-E_0||_F/||D||_F=c/M<10^-5, so both stated tests pass. Yet the dual problem (9) certifies optimum lambda M at A=0,E=D, while this iterate has objective lambda M+cA_1>lambda M. If iterations continue, rule (25) triggers every time because its test remains c/M<10^-5, giving mu_k=1.6^k mu_0 and a finite reciprocal sum. The iterates converge to E_infinity=2.1333cM e_11≈0.6248M e_11 and A_infinity≈0.3752M e_11, still nonoptimal.

Analysis

Evidence
Let bounded, zero-mean LFP x(t) have spectrum Sx(f)>0, and let spikes be conditionally Poisson with intensity λ(t)=μ[1+a x(t)], where positivity is ensured by sufficiently small a. Thus spectral normalization does not establish the claimed firing-rate invariance.

mathematical-error
Evidence type

At M1=2 keV, Equation (12) permits θ1²=1.8×10^-5/2^5=5.625×10^-7, giving δmν≈θ1²M1=1.125×10^-3 eV. From Equation (2), the upper error in sqrt(Δm²_sun) is sqrt(7.88×10^-5)-sqrt(7.65×10^-5)≈1.30×10^-4 eV. The permitted correction is therefore about 8.7 times larger, not smaller; the corresponding threshold is approximately 3.4 keV.

The auxiliary sequence y(t) has opposite signs in its definition and in the proof of Theorem 1. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Section 5.1, equation (15), Lemma 3, and Section 5.2 after equation (20)
Analysis

Evidence
Equation (4) defines Pi(z,alpha)=argmin_x {<z,x>+psi(x)/alpha}. Equation (15) defines y(t)=Pi(-bar{z}(t),alpha), while equation (16) makes bar{z}(t) the positive accumulated average gradient. The spectral-gap lower-bound proof incorrectly replaces a negative singular mode by a positive eigenmode. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Proposition 1 and its proof in Section 6.3, especially equation (36)
Analysis
A symmetric stochastic matrix's second singular value may come from a negative eigenvalue. Lazifying the matrix is not without loss of generality for the proposition's inverse-singular-gap lower bound because it can change that gap by an unbounded factor.
Evidence
Take G=K{m,m}. From equation (8), P_n(G)=(I+A)/(m+1), whose eigenvalues are 1, 1/(m+1) with multiplicity 2m-2, and -(m-1)/(m+1). Replacing P by (I+P)/2 changes the gap from 1-sigma_2(P)=2/(m+1) to m/(2(m+1)), a constant-order gap. Therefore lazification cannot justify the claimed Omega((1-sigma_2(P))^-1)=Omega(m) lower bound for the original matrix.

Findings (1)
A single continuous nonlinear scalar functional cannot, in general, recover every matrix entry or solve a linear system exactly, contrary to the paper's claim that the exact cardinality is one. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 5, page 16, discussion of classes Λ4 and Λ5
Analysis
The assertion fails even for a simple orthogonally invariant class of nonsingular 2×2 matrices. Continuity prevents any real-valued functional from distinguishing all matrices in this class, so one such value cannot determine the exact solution.
Evidence
Let F={Rθ:0≤θ<2π}, where Rθ is the 2×2 rotation matrix, and fix b=(1,0)^T. For any continuous functional L:F→R, define h(θ)=L(Rθ)−L(Rθ+π). Then h is continuous and h(θ+π)=−h(θ), so the intermediate value theorem gives some θ with L(Rθ)=L(Rθ+π). These matrices are distinct and their exact solutions are Rθ^{-1}b and Rθ+π^{-1}b=−Rθ^{-1}b. An algorithm receiving only L(A) must return the same vector for both and therefore cannot solve both exactly. Thus the stated conclusion that the ε-cardinality is one even for ε=0 is false for Λ4.

Authors: J. F. Traub, Henryk Woźniakowski

Tag
mathematical-error

Location
Page 8, Equation 7 and following paragraph
Analysis

Findings (2)
The GPH regression uses a frequency transformation incompatible with the paper's periodogram definition. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 2.3, equations (2) and the preceding periodogram definition
Analysis
The periodogram defines ω_k=k/L and uses exp(-2πi(t-1)ω_k), so the corresponding angular frequency is λ_k=2πω_k. The fractional-difference factor must therefore be |1-exp(-iλ_k)|²=4sin²(πω_k). Equation (2) instead uses 4sin²(ω_k/2), which is appropriate only if ω_k itself is angular frequency. The same ω_k cannot satisfy both conventions.
Evidence
For L=4 and k=1, the periodogram definition gives λ=π/2 and hence |1-exp(-iλ)|²=4sin²(π/4)=2. Equation (2), using ω_k=1/4 as explicitly defined, gives 4sin²(1/8)≈0.0622. Thus the stated regression does not follow from the stated Fourier-frequency convention.

Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Tables 4 and 5
Analysis
Applying Table 4's displayed 99% GPH interval to the stated CalPX sample length places the reported estimate inside the 99% interval, contradicting Table 5's three-star designation.
Evidence
For L=728, N=log2(728)≈9.508 and N^(2/3)≈4.488. Table 4 gives the 99% lower bound as 0.5-exp(-0.73×4.488+2.45)≈0.0624. The reported estimate 0.0667 exceeds this bound and is therefore not significant at 99%. It is below the 95% lower bound, approximately 0.1823, so the displayed formulas support two stars, not three.

arXiv · PDF · 1912.01825v3

Reviewed 2026-07-26 05:12:14+00:00 UTC · gpt-5.6-sol (high)
Community structure in social and biological networks Physics and Astronomy Errors found mathematical-error minor
Review details
Findings (1)
The stated equality between node-independent paths and the minimum vertex-removal cut is false for adjacent vertices. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 2, Section II.A, discussion following Fig. 2
Analysis
The paper states the vertex form of Menger's theorem without the required restriction that the two vertices be nonadjacent. Its statement therefore fails on a simple graph allowed by the paper's definitions.
Evidence
Take a triangle with vertices i, j, and k. There are two node-independent i-j paths: the edge i-j and the path i-k-j. If removal of i and j is forbidden, removing any subset of other vertices cannot disconnect them because edge i-j remains. If endpoint removal is allowed, removing i alone suffices, giving a minimum of 1 rather than 2. Thus the claimed equality is false under either interpretation.
Paper details

Authors: Michelle Girvan, M. E. J. Newman

One minor but demonstrable mathematical error appears in the discussion of node-independent paths; the core community-detection results show no other glaring internal errors.

Findings (5)
Theorem 5.6 omits necessary dependence on the scale of the bounded loss. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Theorem 5.6 and its proof, pages 10–11
Analysis
A loss being bounded does not imply the unit-scale bound used in the proof. Multiplying any bounded loss by a constant preserves boundedness but can violate the theorem's scale-independent inequality.
Evidence
Let A and Y be independent balanced bits, X=Y, R*=Y, and Rhat=1/2 constantly. Under loss l(yhat,y)=10·I[yhat≠y], the optimal equalized-odds predictor Y*=Y has loss 0. Any equalized-odds predictor derived from (Rhat,A) must ignore A statistically and has loss at least 5. The conditional Kolmogorov distance is 1, so Theorem 5.6 asserts loss at most 2√2≈2.83, contradicting the lower bound 5.
Equation (4.5) is not the expected loss because it omits outcome prevalences. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Equation (4.5), page 9
Analysis
For common false-positive and true-positive rates γ0 and γ1, expected loss includes factors Pr(Y=0) and Pr(Y=1). These factors cannot be discarded for the previously defined per-example loss.
Evidence
With zero loss for correct predictions, expected loss is Pr(Y=0)γ0·l(1,0)+Pr(Y=1)(1−γ1)·l(0,1), not Equation (4.5). For unit error costs and Pr(Y=1)=0.99, suppose identical group-conditional ROC regions contain (0.2,0.8) and (1,1). Equation (4.5) prefers (0.2,0.8), assigning costs 0.4 versus 1. Actual errors are 0.20 versus 0.01, so the true optimum is (1,1).
The deterministic correction from R* to Re uses a threshold with the wrong sign. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Section 6.2, page 14
Analysis
The paper states R*=Re+A and then claims I[Re>t]=I[R*>tA] with tA=t−A. Direct substitution gives the opposite sign.
Evidence
From Re=R*−A, Re>t is equivalent to R*>t+A. Therefore the correct group threshold is tA=t+A, not t−A.
The proof of Proposition 4.4 uses an invalid probability factorization. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Proof of Proposition 4.4, page 7
Analysis
The proof replaces probabilities conditioned on whether the derived predictor matches the original predictor by products of unconditional probabilities. The matching event generally depends on the original prediction and protected group.
Evidence
Let A be an independent bit, Y be balanced, Yhat=Y, and the derived predictor Ye=1 constantly. For y'=y=1, the displayed left side is Pr(Ye=1,Y=1)=1/2. Its displayed final expression is Pr(Yhat=1,Y=1)Pr(Ye=Yhat)+Pr(Yhat=0,Y=1)Pr(Ye≠Yhat)=(1/2)(1/2)+0=1/4. Thus the asserted equality is false.

Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 2, paragraph following Definition 2.1, page 3
Analysis
Equalized odds equalizes conditional true- and false-positive rates, but overall accuracy also depends on each group's outcome prevalence.
Evidence
Take equalized rates TPR=0.9 and FPR=0.2 in both groups, with Pr(Y=1|A=0)=0.9 and Pr(Y=1|A=1)=0.1. Equalized odds holds, but accuracies are 0.9·0.9+0.8·0.1=0.89 and 0.9·0.1+0.8·0.9=0.81.

Findings (1)
The claimed stability interval ignores the mixed second derivative of the two-domain free energy. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Supplementary section S14, Eq. S1–S4 and Table S1, page 22
Analysis
The paper declares configurations stable when both listed diagonal second derivatives are positive. For a two-variable free energy, stability instead requires the full Hessian to be positive definite. Eq. S1 has a nonzero mixed derivative, so positivity of the two diagonal entries is insufficient.
Evidence
Let C=J M1 M2−D, so Eq. S1 contains C cos(θ1−θ2). At (θ1,θ2)=(0,0), the Hessian is [[−C+B M1+2K M1, C],[C,−C+B M2+2K M2]]. Choose M1=2, M2=1, J=1, D=1, K=1, and B=−0.9. These satisfy M1>M2, J>0, K>0, and J M1 M2>D. They also place B within the paper's stated interval C/M2−2K<B<C/M2+2K, namely −1<B<3. Both listed diagonal derivatives are positive: 1.2 and 0.1. However, the Hessian determinant is 1.2×0.1−1²=−0.88, so one eigenvalue is negative and (0,0) is a saddle, not a stable configuration.

Clipping the symmetric cubic map does not make it a valid rank transmutation map for every γ. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type

Location
Section 2.6.1, Equation (14)
Analysis
The paper requires rank transmutation maps to be monotone, then claims that clipping Equation (14) to [0,1] produces a valid map for all γ. Clipping does not repair interior nonmonotonicity.
Evidence
Set γ=-10. Before clipping, h(u)=u-10u(1-u)(u-1/2). Then h(0.4)=0.64 and h(0.6)=0.36. Both values already lie in [0,1], so clipping leaves them unchanged. Since 0.4<0.6 but G_R12(0.4)>G_R12(0.6), the map is not monotone and therefore is not a valid RTM under the paper’s definition.
Equation (12) does not produce the claimed mean-zero standardized skew-normal density. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Section 2.5, Equations (11)–(12)
Analysis

Evidence
For f2(x)=φ(x)[1+λ-2λΦ(x)], integration by parts gives ∫xφ(x)Φ(x)dx=∫φ(x)^2dx=1/(2√π), hence E[X]=-λ/√π. Also E[X²]=1, so σ²=1-λ²/π. A mean-zero density must therefore be σf2(σx-λ/√π,λ). The printed σf2(σx-λ/π,λ) instead has mean (λ/π-λ/√π)/σ, which is nonzero for λ≠0.

Counterfactual Fairness Statistics Errors found mathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (3)
Lemma 2's stated conditions do not imply demographic parity. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Supplementary Section S2, Lemma 2, page 13
Analysis
The assumptions control observables conditional on the predictor's background variables but never require those background variables to be independent of A. Consequently, their distribution can vary with A and transmit that dependence to the predictor.
Evidence
Let U be Bernoulli(1/2), with structural equations A=U and X=U, and define Y-hat=(U+X)/2=U. The background variable U is determined by A and X. The sole observable predictor input X is independent of A conditional on U because both are then deterministic. Thus every stated premise of Lemma 2 holds. Nevertheless, P(Y-hat=1|A=1)=1 while P(Y-hat=1|A=0)=0, violating demographic parity.
The claim that every nonconstant estimator depending only on X is counterfactually unfair in the Red Car structure is false. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Supplementary Section S3, immediately after Lemma 3, page 15
Analysis
Changing X under an intervention on A need not change a nonconstant function of X; the function can be invariant to the particular shift induced by A.
Evidence
Let A be Bernoulli with attainable values 0 and 1, U be independent Uniform(0,1), X=A+U, and Y=sin(2piU). This has exactly the structure A→X←U→Y. The nonconstant X-only predictor Y-hat(X)=sin(2piX) satisfies Y-hat under do(A=0)=sin(2piU)=Y-hat under do(A=1), because sin(2pi(U+1))=sin(2piU). It is therefore counterfactually fair, contrary to the universal claim.
The average causal-effect calculation for X=A+U has its sign reversed. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Supplementary Section S1, derivation following Equation (2), page 12
Analysis
Substitution into the paper's ordered difference gives the negative of the reported expression.
Evidence
With Y-hat=lambda*U and X=A+U, conditioning on X=x after do(A=a) gives U=x-a. Therefore lambda(x-a)-lambda(x-a0)=lambda(a0-a), whereas the paper reports lambda(a-a0). The nonzero-effect conclusion remains valid, but the stated value has the wrong sign.
Paper details

Authors: Matt J. Kusner, Joshua R. Loftus, Chris Russell, Ricardo Silva

Two explicit mathematical claims admit counterexamples, and one displayed causal-effect calculation has a reversed sign.

Tag

Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Theorem 1.1 and Section 3.2, especially Lemma 3.1 and equations (3.2)–(3.3)
Analysis
Theorem 1.1 states C1=4/(1−δS−θS,2S). In the proof, T01 has up to 2S elements, so Lemma 3.1 necessarily uses δ=δ2S, as the paper's notation also specifies. Combining the lemma with the cone and residual constraints therefore gives a denominator 1−δ2S−θS,2S, not 1−δS−θS,2S. The claimed saturation cutoff (R/σ)^(1/s) after Theorem 1.3 is false. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 1.5, discussion following equations (1.18)–(1.19)
Analysis
The decay condition |β(k)|≤Rk^(−1/s) has its natural crossover at S≈(R/σ)^s, not (R/σ)^(1/s). Consequently, parameters can satisfy the paper's alleged saturation condition while the bound (1.18) still attains the rate (1.19).
Evidence
Let s=1/2, σ=1, R=m², r=3/2, and S*=2m, using an orthonormal design with p=n≥4m so all restricted constants vanish. For m≥2, S*≤(R/σ)^(1/s)=m^4, placing this case in the paper's claimed saturation regime. Yet choosing S≈3^(1/4)m≤S* in (1.18) gives S+R²S^(−3)=Θ(m), exactly the order of (1.19), R^(2/(2r+1))=R^(1/2)=m. Thus there is enough admissible sparsity to attain the stated minimax order.

Findings (1)
Quotienting the entire oblique manifold by left orthogonal transformations does not yield the elliptope of matrices having rank exactly n. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 1, first bullet, pages 1–2
Analysis
The paper defines the oblique manifold using only unit-column constraints, then claims its orthogonal quotient yields Gram matrices of rank n. Those constraints do not require the factor to have full row rank, while the rank of its Gram matrix equals the factor's rank.
Evidence
Set n=m=2 and X=[[1,1],[0,0]]. Both columns have unit Euclidean norm, so X belongs to the paper's oblique manifold. However, Y=X^T X=[[1,1],[1,1]] has diagonal (1,1), is positive semidefinite, and has rank 1 rather than the claimed rank n=2. Orthogonal transformations QX preserve this Gram matrix and cannot remove the counterexample. A full-row-rank restriction is necessary for the asserted fixed-rank quotient.

Findings (4)
The stated HSS formula cannot produce several HSS values reported in Table 3. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Section 5.2, HSS definition; Table 3
Analysis
For nonnegative contingency-table counts, the printed formula is bounded above by 0.5, whereas Table 3 reports many HSS values above 0.5.
Evidence
Writing TP=a, FN=b, FP=c, TN=d, the stated denominator is (a+b)(b+d)+(a+c)(c+d)=2ad+ab+b^2+bd+ac+c^2+cd, which is at least 2ad. Its numerator ad-bc is at most ad, so the stated HSS is at most 0.5. Table 3 nevertheless reports values such as 0.6756 for offline ConvGRU-nobal. Thus the equation and table cannot both be correct.

The stated all-zero initialization prevents the trajectory-generating network from learning input-dependent flows. (major, high confidence)
Tag
experimental-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix A-B and Equation 4
Analysis
Appendix A initializes every weight and bias in the two-layer structure-generating network to zero. Under gradient-based training, this leaves both convolutional weight matrices permanently zero, allowing only a spatially constant output bias to change.
Evidence
Let gamma(q)=W2 f(W1q+b1)+b2. Initially W1=W2=b1=b2=0, so the hidden activation is zero. Therefore dL/dW2=(dL/dgamma)f(0)^T=0 and dL/dW1=W2^T(dL/dgamma)f'(0)q^T=0. Although b2 may update, the hidden activation remains zero, so both weight gradients remain zero thereafter. Gamma consequently cannot depend on location, timestamp, input, or state as claimed.
A positive unit-sum dynamic filter does not necessarily reduce the maximum value or prevent pixels from increasing. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4, discussion following Table 1
Analysis
The explanation given for DFN's poor performance makes false universal claims about convex filtering.
Evidence
For any positive weights summing to one, a constant neighborhood with value c produces sum_i w_i c=c, so its maximum does not get smaller. Likewise, a pixel initially equal to zero increases whenever its neighborhood contains a positive value receiving positive weight. Both cases satisfy the paper's stated positive, unit-sum filter assumption.

The sub-Gaussian tail assumption does not imply the moment-generating-function bound used to prove Theorem 1.3. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4, derivation following Equation (28), especially lines 1222-1238
Analysis

Location

Analysis

The listed reaction ³He(⁴He,γ)⁷Li violates electric-charge conservation. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
factual-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Section 1.1, page 2, discussion preceding Equation 1.1
Analysis
The reaction cannot occur as written under the Standard Model physics assumed by the paper. It is likely a typographical duplication of the correctly listed ³He(⁴He,γ)⁷Be channel, but the printed claim is nevertheless false.
Evidence
The initial nuclei have total charge Z = 2 + 2 = 4. The proposed products have Z = 3 for ⁷Li and Z = 0 for the photon, totaling 3. The paper later correctly identifies ³He(⁴He,γ)⁷Be, whose product has Z = 4, as the dominant mass-7 production channel.

High-Resolution Image Synthesis with Latent Diffusion Models Computer Science Errors found experimental-errormathematical-error majorminor

Findings (3)
The ELBO incorrectly treats the final reconstruction step as an ordinary posterior KL term. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix B, equations (9), (11), and (13), pages 15–16
Analysis
Equation (9) sums posterior KL terms through t=1. At t=1, however, x_{t-1}=x0 is the observed datum, so q(x0|x1,x0) is a point mass rather than the nondegenerate Gaussian required by the subsequent KL-to-MSE derivation. Accordingly, equation (13)'s t=1 weight is undefined or infinite. This endpoint must instead be handled as a separate reconstruction likelihood term.
Evidence
For the uncorrupted endpoint, alpha_0=1 and sigma_0=0. Thus equation (11)'s posterior variance at t=1 is proportional to sigma_0^2=0, while equation (13) contains SNR(0)-SNR(1), with SNR(0)=alpha_0^2/sigma_0^2=infinity. Therefore the t=1 KL cannot equal the finite squared-error term asserted in equation (13).
The image-guidance update has the opposite sign from the conditional score correction. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix C, equation (16), page 18
Analysis
Equation (16) adds the likelihood gradient to the predicted noise. Noise prediction has the opposite sign from score prediction, so conditioning requires subtracting this gradient from epsilon, not adding it.
Evidence
From equations (4) and (14), the score corresponding to epsilon prediction is -epsilon_theta/sigma_t. Conditional guidance requires score_cond=score_uncond+grad log p_Phi(y|z_t). Hence -epsilon_hat/sigma_t=-epsilon_theta/sigma_t+grad log p_Phi, giving epsilon_hat=epsilon_theta-sigma_t grad log p_Phi. Equation (16) instead uses a plus sign with sigma_t=sqrt(1-alpha_t^2), reversing the guidance direction.

Generator compute is 60 V100-days and classifier compute is absent, yielding 60+0=60 V100-days. The reported overall compute is 55 V100-days.

Auto-Encoding Variational Bayes Statistics Errors found mathematical-error minor
Review details
Findings (1)
The paper incorrectly states that maximizing mutual information is equivalent to maximizing conditional entropy. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 6, Section 4, lines 327-332
Analysis
For a fixed data distribution, I(X;Z)=H(X)-H(X|Z), so maximizing mutual information requires minimizing, not maximizing, H(X|Z). The expected conditional log-likelihood lower-bounds -H(X|Z), not H(X|Z). This is a definite sign error, although it does not affect the paper's principal derivations.
Evidence
Let X be a fair binary variable. If Z=X, then I(X;Z)=1 bit and H(X|Z)=0. If Z is independent of X, then I(X;Z)=0 while H(X|Z)=1 bit. Thus maximizing conditional entropy favors the representation with lower mutual information, directly contradicting the stated equivalence.
Paper details

Authors: Diederik P. Kingma, Max Welling

One demonstrable sign error appears in the related-work discussion; the central SGVB and AEVB derivations show no glaring internal error.

Findings (3)
The momentum equation implements the reverse of the stated schedule. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Appendix A.1, page 8, momentum schedule equation
Analysis
The prose says momentum increases linearly from 0.5 to 0.99 over 500 epochs, but the displayed equation starts at 0.99 and decreases toward 0.5.
Evidence
The equation is p^t=(t/T)p_i+(1-t/T)p_f for t<T, with p_i=0.5 and p_f=0.99. Therefore p^0=0.99 and p^t approaches 0.5 as t approaches 500, exactly opposite to the stated schedule.

Findings (2)
Batch Normalization is not invariant to an arbitrary scalar rescaling as claimed. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 3.3, page 5, displayed equations beginning BN(Wu) = BN((aW)u)
Analysis
The claimed equality contradicts Algorithm 1's definition. Consequently, the stated Jacobian equalities and conclusion that parameter scale does not affect gradient propagation do not follow exactly.
Evidence
Set gamma=1, beta=0 and let Wu over a batch be (-1,1). For a=-1, Algorithm 1 gives BN(Wu)=(-1,1)/sqrt(1+epsilon), whereas BN((aW)u)=(1,-1)/sqrt(1+epsilon), so they are unequal. Even restricting a to positive values does not rescue the exact claim when epsilon>0: for a=2, the outputs are (-1,1)/sqrt(1+epsilon) and (-2,2)/sqrt(4+epsilon), respectively.
The stated output depths of Inception modules 4c and 4d disagree with their listed branch widths. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
experimental-error
Evidence type
arithmetic-error
Location
Appendix, Figure 5, page 11, rows inception (4c) and inception (4d)
Analysis
The table describes concatenated Inception branches, so output depth is the sum of the branch output widths; reduction widths are not outputs. The listed values do not sum to the stated depth.
Evidence
For 4c, the output branches have widths 160, 160, 160, and 128, totaling 608 rather than the stated 576. For 4d, they have widths 96, 192, 192, and 128, again totaling 608 rather than 576.
Paper details

Authors: Sergey Ioffe, Christian Szegedy

Two demonstrable internal errors were found: an incorrect scale-invariance identity and inconsistent channel arithmetic in the architecture table.

arXiv · PDF · 1502.03167v3

Reviewed 2026-07-26 02:37:42+00:00 UTC · gpt-5.6-sol (high)
Generative Adversarial Networks Statistics Errors found logical-errormathematical-error major
Review details
Findings (2)
The minimax game does not have a unique solution in the space of arbitrary functions G and D. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Abstract; Section 4.1, Proposition 1 and Theorem 1
Analysis
The abstract claims a unique functional solution, but Theorem 1 establishes uniqueness only of the generated distribution pg. Distinct generators can induce the same optimal distribution. Proposition 1 also states that D need not be defined outside the union of the two distributions' supports, contradicting the claim that the unique solution has D equal to 1/2 everywhere.
Evidence
Let z be uniform on [0,1] and pdata be uniform on [0,1]. Both G1(z)=z and G2(z)=1-z are distinct functions inducing pg=pdata, and both attain C(G)=-log 4 with D=1/2 on the support. Moreover, D may take arbitrary values outside [0,1] without affecting the objective. Hence multiple functional pairs (G,D) solve the game.
Merely improving the criterion at each sufficiently small update does not imply that pg converges to pdata. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 4.2, Proposition 2 and its proof
Analysis
The proof incorrectly infers convergence from convexity, a unique optimum, and sufficiently small improving updates. Convergence additionally requires conditions such as a suitable nonsummable step-size schedule. Arbitrarily small, strictly improving gradient steps can have finite total displacement and stop short of the optimum.
Evidence
Take a two-point space with pdata(1)=1/2 and pg,n(1)=qn, where qn=0.8+10^-6*2^-n. For q>1/2, the virtual criterion has derivative C'(q)=log(q(1.5-q)/((0.5+q)(1-q)))>0. Thus every update qn+1<qn strictly improves C and can be written as a positive gradient-descent step; the updates are arbitrarily small and D may be optimized exactly at every step. Nevertheless qn converges to 0.8 rather than 0.5, so pg does not converge to pdata.
Paper details

Authors: Ian J. Goodfellow, Jean Pouget-Abadie, Mehdi Mirza, Bing Xu, David Warde-Farley, Sherjil Ozair, Aaron Courville, Yoshua Bengio

The paper contains a false uniqueness claim and an invalid convergence proposition.

arXiv · PDF · 1406.2661v1

Reviewed 2026-07-26 02:34:58+00:00 UTC · gpt-5.6-sol (high)
Sequence to Sequence Learning with Neural Networks Computer Science Errors found logical-error minor
Review details
Findings (1)
The paper incorrectly claims that supervised backpropagation will find good parameters whenever such parameters exist and the training data are sufficient. (minor, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Page 1, Introduction
Analysis
Existence of a good parameter setting does not imply that backpropagation can reach it. This is a concrete optimization failure, not merely an unsupported generalization, although it does not invalidate the paper's experiments.
Evidence
Consider the two-layer scalar network f(x)=w2w1x with squared loss on the single example x=1, y=1. Parameters w1=w2=1 achieve zero loss. At initialization w1=w2=0, however, both derivatives are zero: dL/dw1=2(w1w2-1)w2=0 and dL/dw2=2(w1w2-1)w1=0. Backpropagation therefore never leaves the zero-loss-gradient initialization and cannot find the existing solution, contradicting the statement that it 'will find these parameters.' The construction extends directly to arbitrarily wide deep linear networks initialized with zero layer matrices.
Paper details

Authors: Ilya Sutskever, Oriol Vinyals, Quoc V. Le

One demonstrable logical error appears in the introductory optimization claim; no concrete errors were found in the reported arithmetic or core sequence-to-sequence formulation.

Mastering Chess and Shogi by Self-Play with a General Reinforcement Learning Algorithm Computer Science Errors found factual-error major
Review details
Findings (1)
AlphaZero is claimed to use no domain knowledge except game rules, but the Methods explicitly identify and use non-rule domain knowledge. (major, high confidence)
Tag
factual-error
Evidence type
direct-contradiction
Location
Abstract and conclusion; Methods, “Domain Knowledge,” items 1, 4, and 5
Analysis
The abstract says AlphaZero was given “no domain knowledge except the game rules.” However, the paper's own “Domain Knowledge” section lists additional inputs: an architecture matched to the board's grid structure, exploration-noise scaling based on the typical number of legal moves, and maximum game lengths determined from typical game length. The latter two are empirical domain statistics, not game rules. Because the paper itself classifies these items as domain knowledge, both statements cannot be true as written.
Evidence
The abstract restricts domain knowledge to game rules. Methods item 4 states, “The typical number of legal moves is used to scale the exploration noise,” and item 5 states that maximum steps were “determined by typical game length.” The section concludes that AlphaZero used no domain knowledge “beyond the points listed above,” expressly acknowledging those additional forms of domain knowledge.

Analysis

arXiv · PDF · 1301.3781v3

Reviewed 2026-07-26 01:56:00+00:00 UTC · gpt-5.6-sol (high)
Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization Computer Science Errors found factual-errorlogical-errormathematical-error majorminor
Review details
Findings (6)
Lemma 10.3 is false under its stated assumptions. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Appendix, Lemma 10.3
Analysis
The lemma's bound fails even in one dimension with one timestep.
Evidence
Set T=d=1 and g_1=0.1, with valid bounds G=G_infinity=0.1. The claimed left side is sqrt(g_1^2)=0.1, while the right side is 2 G_infinity ||g_1||_2=2(0.1)(0.1)=0.02. Thus 0.1 <= 0.02 is false. Lemma 10.4 and the main regret proof subsequently invoke this lemma.
The regret proof incorrectly telescopes coefficients that need not be nonnegative. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Appendix, proof of Theorem 10.5, equations immediately before the bound beginning 'From the assumption'
Analysis
The proof replaces squared distances by their upper bound inside terms weighted by differences of sqrt(v_hat_t)/alpha_t. That replacement preserves an upper bound only when those differences are nonnegative, but Adam does not ensure this.
Evidence
Take beta_2=0.9, g_1=1, and g_2=0. Then v_hat_1=1 and v_hat_2=0.09/0.19=9/19. With alpha_t=alpha/sqrt(t), sqrt(v_hat_1)/alpha_1=1/alpha, whereas sqrt(v_hat_2)/alpha_2=sqrt(18/19)/alpha<1/alpha. The coefficient difference at t=2 is therefore negative. For a negative coefficient, replacing a squared distance x<=D^2 by D^2 reverses the required inequality, so the displayed regret bound does not follow.
The proof applies the iterate-distance assumption to the comparator theta-star without justification. (major, high confidence)
Tag
logical-error
Evidence type
invalid-derivation
Location
Theorem 10.5 and its proof, statement 'From the assumption, ||theta_t-theta-star||_2 <= D'
Analysis
The theorem assumes only that pairs of parameters generated by Adam are within D. The regret comparator theta-star is an arbitrary best fixed point in X and need not be a generated iterate.
Evidence
A sequence with every generated iterate equal to 0 satisfies the stated pairwise condition with D=0, while a comparator theta-star=1 has distance 1 from every iterate. Hence the proof's asserted implication does not follow from the theorem's assumptions.
Section 2.1's claimed bound |Delta_t| <= alpha in its 'otherwise' parameter regime is false. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 2.1, Adam's update rule
Analysis
Permitted decay rates can make the first-moment estimate decay much more slowly than the square root of the second-moment estimate.
Evidence
Use epsilon=0 as assumed in the section, beta_1=0.99, beta_2=0.9, g_1=1, and g_2 through g_100=0. Since 1-beta_1=0.01<sqrt(1-beta_2), the paper claims |Delta_100|<=alpha. Instead, m_hat_100=0.01(0.99^99)/(1-0.99^100) approximately 0.00583 and v_hat_100=0.1(0.9^99)/(1-0.9^100) approximately 2.95e-6. Therefore |m_hat_100|/sqrt(v_hat_100) approximately 3.39, giving |Delta_100| approximately 3.39 alpha.
The claimed universal AdaMax update bound |Delta_t| <= alpha is false. (major, high confidence)
Tag
mathematical-error
Evidence type
counterexample
Location
Section 7.1, immediately after Equation 12
Analysis
No stated condition prevents AdaMax's momentum from decaying more slowly than its infinity-norm accumulator.
Evidence
Use beta_1=0.99, beta_2=0.9, g_1=1, and g_2 through g_100=0, all allowed by Algorithm 2. Then m_hat_100 approximately 0.00583, while u_100=0.9^99 approximately 2.95e-5. Hence |Delta_100|/alpha=|m_hat_100|/u_100 approximately 197.6, contradicting the asserted bound.

Evidence

Section 5 says beta_2 close to 1 is 'required in case of sparse gradients,' and Section 6.4 repeats that values close to 1 are required for robustness to sparse gradients. Both prescriptions cannot be true as written.
